H_{2}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
is open and that the relative topology given to the subset
H_{2}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
coincides with the usual topology of
H_{2}
.

Lemma 3.47
.

For any pair
A,B\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
, there is a homeomorphic automorphism
\varphi\colon{\overline{H_{2}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying
\varphi(A)=B
.

Proof.

This can be easily seen by considering
U_{2}
instead of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Indeed, if
V,V^{\prime}\in U_{2}
, then the mapping
\varphi\colon U_{2}\to U_{2}
defined by
U\mapsto V^{\prime}V^{*}U
,
U\in U_{2}
, is clearly a homeomorphism satisfying
\varphi(V)=V^{\prime}
and

U\sim U^{\prime}\iff\varphi(U)\sim\varphi(U^{\prime})

for every pair
U,U^{\prime}\in U_{2}
.
∎

Remark 3.48
.

In fact, it turns out that every automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is a homeomorphism, but we will not need such a general fact.

Lemma 3.49
.

The interior of a cone in
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is empty.
Let
\ell\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a line and
\mathcal{U}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
an open subset.
If
\ell\cap\mathcal{U}
is nonempty, then it has infinitely many points.

Proof.

Observe that a unitary
U\in U_{2}
is coherent to
I
if and only if one of the eigenvalues of
U
is
1
.
It is easily seen that the set of all such unitaries has empty interior in
U_{2}
.
This together with Lemma
3.47
proves the first statement.
Let us show the second statement.
By Lemma
3.47
again, it suffices to consider the case
0\in\ell\cap\mathcal{U}
.
Then
\ell=\{tP\,:\,t\in{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}\}
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, and
H_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}
is an open neighborhood of
0
in
H_{2}
. It follows that
\ell\cap\mathcal{U}
has infinitely many points.
∎

Lemma 3.50
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{A}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
and assume that
\mathcal{U}
is open in
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map.
Assume that
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in\mathcal{U}
.
If
A\in\mathcal{A}
satisfies
\mathcal{C}_{A}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset
, then
\varphi(A)=A
.

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathcal{A}
satisfy
\mathcal{C}_{A}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset
.
Since
\mathcal{U}
is open and the interior of a line is empty, we may take a pair of distinct lines
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
passing through
A
such that
\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}
and
\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}
are nonempty.
Then, by the openness of
\mathcal{U}
again we see that
\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}
and
\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}
have infinitely many points.
Observe that
\varphi(\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{A})
and
\varphi(\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{A})
are coherent sets containing
\varphi(\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{U})=\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}
and
\varphi(\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{U})=\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}
, respectively.
It follows that
\varphi(\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{A})\subset\ell_{1}
and
\varphi(\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{A})\subset\ell_{2}
.
Thus we conclude that

\varphi(A)\in\varphi(\ell_{1}\cap\mathcal{A})\cap\varphi(\ell_{2}\cap\mathcal{A})\subset\ell_{1}\cap\ell_{2}=\{A\}.

∎

Lemma 3.51
.

Let
A,B,C\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B<C
. Let
\varphi\colon[A,C]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver. Assume that
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[A,B]
. Then
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[A,C]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.18
, we may, and we will assume that
A=0
and
C=I
without loss of generality.
Hence, we are considering
B\in H_{2}
with
0<B<I
and a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[0,B]
.
Let
t_{0}
be the smaller eigenvalue of
B
. Then
0<t_{0}<1
.

We consider the subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}:=(0,t_{0}I)\subset[0,I]
, which is open in
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Note that
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subset[0,B]
.
If one of the eigenvalues of a matrix
A\in[0,I]
lies in
(0,t_{0})
, then
\mathcal{C}_{A}\cap\mathcal{U}_{1}
is clearly nonempty, so Lemma
3.50
implies
\varphi(A)=A
.
Next we define the open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
to be the set formed of all matrices
X
in
(0,I)
such that
\sigma(X)\cap(0,t_{0})\neq\emptyset
.
We already know that
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
.
If
A\in[0,I]\setminus(\{0,I\}\cup\mathcal{P})
, then it is easy to see that
\mathcal{C}_{A}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
is nonempty, thus we obtain
\varphi(A)=A
again by Lemma
3.50
.
We know
\varphi(0)=0
from the assumption.
From
\varphi(P+tP^{\perp})=P+tP^{\perp}
for every
t\in(0,1)
and
P\in\mathcal{P}
, it clearly follows that
\varphi(I)=I
. (Indeed, we have
\varphi(I)\in\ell_{P+tP^{\perp},P+sP^{\perp}}=\ell_{P,I}
for every
0<t<s<1
and
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.) Finally, for
P\in\mathcal{P}
we know that
\varphi(P)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\varphi(0),\varphi(I)}=\mathcal{P}
and
\varphi(P)\sim\varphi((1/2)P)=(1/2)P
, and therefore
\varphi(P)=P
.
∎

Corollary 3.52
.

Let
A,B,C\in H_{2}
with
A<B<C
. Let
\varphi\colon[A,C]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver. Assume that
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[B,C]
. Then
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[A,C]
.

Proof.

We define a coherency preserver
\varphi_{1}\colon[0,C-A]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\varphi_{1}(X)=C-\varphi(C-X),\ \ \ X\in[0,C-A].

It is straightforward to check that the restriction of
\varphi_{1}
to
[0,C-B]
is the identity map. By Lemma
3.51
,
\varphi_{1}
is the identity on the whole matrix interval
[0,C-A]
, which is equivalent to
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[A,C]
.
∎

In the proof of the next corollary, we will use the following simple fact: Let
\mathcal{U}\subset H_{2}
be an open subset and
A\in\mathcal{U}
. Then there exist
B,C\in H_{2}
such that
B<A<C
and
[B,C]\subset\mathcal{U}
. Indeed, we only need to observe that for each
\varepsilon>0
we have
[A-\varepsilon I,A+\varepsilon I]=\{D\in H_{2}\,:\,\|D-A\|\leq\varepsilon\}
.

Corollary 3.53
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
with
A<B
. Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}

be a coherency preserver. Assume that
the set
\{X\in[A,B]\,:\,\varphi(X)=X\}
as a subset of
H_{2}
has nonempty interior.
Then
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[A,B]
.

Proof.

By the assumption together with the remark above, we may take
C,D\in(A,B)
with
C<D
such that
\varphi(X)=X
for every
X\in[C,D]
.
By Lemma
3.51
,
\varphi
is the identity on the matrix interval
[C,B]
. By Corollary
3.52
,
\varphi
is the identity on the whole matrix interval
[A,B]
.
∎

Corollary 3.54
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
with
A<B
. Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}

be a coherency preserver.
Assume that there exists an automorphism
\psi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}

such that the set
\{X\in[A,B]\,:\,\varphi(X)=\psi(X)\}
as a subset of
H_{2}
has nonempty interior.
Then
\varphi(X)=\psi(X)
for every
X\in[A,B]
.

Proof.

After introducing a new coherency preserver defined by

X\mapsto\psi^{-1}(\varphi(X)),\ \ \ X\in[A,B],

we may assume with no loss of generality that
\psi
is the identity. Then the desired conclusion follows directly from Corollary
3.53
.
∎

We continue with an identity-type theorem.

Theorem 3.55
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a nonempty connected open subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
and let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a nonempty open subset of
\mathcal{A}
. If an automorphism
\psi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}

satisfies
\varphi(X)=\psi(X)
for every
X\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\varphi(X)=\psi(X)
for every
X\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

We denote by
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{A}
the set of all points
A\in\mathcal{A}
with the property that

\varphi(X)=\psi(X)
for every
X
in some neighborhood of
A
. It is trivial to see that
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{L}
and that
\mathcal{L}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{A}
.
It remains to prove that
\mathcal{L}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{A}
.

Hence, assume that
A\in\mathcal{A}
and that there exists a sequence
A_{n}\in\mathcal{L}
,
n\geq 1
, such that
\lim A_{n}=A
.
By Lemma
3.47
, there is a homeomorphic automorphism
\psi_{1}\colon{\overline{H_{2}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying
\psi_{1}(A)=0
.
Thus
\psi_{1}(A_{n})
tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
.
Let
\varepsilon
be a positive number such that
[-\varepsilon I,\varepsilon I]\subset\psi_{1}(\mathcal{A})
. There exists a positive integer
m
such that
\psi_{1}(A_{m})\in(-\varepsilon I,\varepsilon I)
.
By Corollary
3.54
, we have

\varphi\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}(X)=\psi\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}(X)
for every
X\in[-\varepsilon I,\varepsilon I]
. Thus, we get
A\in\mathcal{L}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.56
.

The assumption of openness in Theorem
3.55
is essential.
For example, since
tI\not\sim sI
for distinct
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
, we see that any mapping from
\{tI\,:\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
to
{\overline{H_{2}}}
preserves coherency even when
\varphi(tI)=tI
for every
t>0
.

The assumption of connectedness is also essential.
For example, consider the mapping
\varphi\colon H_{2}^{++}\cup H_{2}^{--}\to H_{2}
defined by

\varphi(X)=X,\ \ X\in H_{2}^{++},\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi(H_{2}^{--})=\{0\}.

Since
X\not\sim Y
for any
X\in H_{2}^{++}
and
Y\in H_{2}^{--}
, we see that
\varphi
preserves coherency.
The identity mapping on
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is an automorphism that extends
\varphi|_{H_{2}^{++}}
, but
\varphi
is not the identity mapping on
H_{2}^{--}
.

We close this section with two lemmas whose motivation is close to the identity theorem.

Lemma 3.57
.

Let
A,B\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfy
d(A,B)=2
.
If
C\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
has the property
C\sim X
for every
X\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
then
C\in\{A,B\}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
A=0
and
B={\widehat{\infty}}
.
In this case, we have
\mathcal{S}_{A,B}=\{\infty P\,:\,P\in\mathcal{P}\}
, and it is easy to get to the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 3.58
.

Let
A\in H_{2}
,
0<A\leq I
, and
B\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
. Assume that for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
and every real
s\in[0,1]
we have

A\sim sP\Rightarrow B\sim sP.

Then
B=A
or
B=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.57
, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\subset\{sP\,:\,s\in[0,1],P\in\mathcal{P}\}
.
Using the standard automorphism
X\mapsto A^{1/2}XA^{1/2}
,
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
, we see

\mathcal{S}_{0,A}=\{A^{1/2}PA^{1/2}\,:\,P\in\mathcal{S}_{0,I}\}.

Observe that for
P\in\mathcal{S}_{0,I}=\mathcal{P}
the norm of the rank-one positive operator
A^{1/2}PA^{1/2}
is at most
1
,
which leads to the desired claim.
∎

Part II
Main results and their proofs

4.
Standard coherency preservers

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
and
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
a map. We say that
\varphi
is a
standard coherency preserver
if
\varphi
extends to a standard automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
The main result of the current section is the following one.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a connected open subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
and
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
a coherency preserving map. Assume that there exist
A,B,C\in\mathcal{A}\cap H_{2}
such that

•

A<C<B
,

•

[A,B]\subset\mathcal{A}
, and

•

the triple
\varphi(A),\varphi(C),\varphi(B)
is in timelike position.

Then
\varphi
is standard.

4.1.
Proof of Theorem A

We first show the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver satisfying

(4.1)

\varphi(0)=0,\ \ \ \varphi((1/2)I)=(1/2)I,\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \varphi(I)=I.

Then there exists a unitary
U\in U_{2}
satisfying either

\varphi(X)=UXU^{*},\ \ \ X\in[0,I],

or

\varphi(X)=UX^{t}U^{*},\ \ \ X\in[0,I].

To prove this, we assume that
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is a coherency preserver satisfying (
4.1
).
By Lemma
3.12
, we have
\varphi(\mathcal{P})\subset\mathcal{P}
.
Lemma
3.12
also shows that the triple
0,(1/2)I,I
is not contained in any cone.

We fix
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Since
0,(1/2)I,I\in\varphi({\square}_{P})
, where
{\square}_{P}
is defined as in (
3.13
), Lemma
3.35
yields that
\varphi({\square}_{P})
is contained in some surface. By Lemma
3.33
, we have

\varphi({\square}_{P})\subset\{a\varphi(P)+b\varphi(P)^{\perp}\,:\,a,b\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\}.

Claim 4.2
.

The equation

(4.2)

\varphi(aP+bP^{\perp})=a\varphi(P)+b\varphi(P)^{\perp}

holds for any
a,b\in\{0,1/2,1\}
.

Proof.

We have
\varphi((1/2)P)\sim\varphi((1/2)I)=(1/2)I
and
\varphi((1/2)P)\sim\varphi(0)=0
, and by a simple modification of Lemma
3.12
we see that
\varphi((1/2)P)=(1/2)Q
for some
Q\in\mathcal{P}
. But
\varphi((1/2)P)\sim\varphi(P)\in\mathcal{P}
and therefore,

\varphi((1/2)P)=(1/2)\varphi(P).

We continue with
\varphi((1/2)P+P^{\perp})
. By

(1/2)P+P^{\perp}\sim(1/2)I,I,(1/2)P,

we get

\varphi((1/2)P+P^{\perp})\sim(1/2)I,I,(1/2)\varphi(P).

Using Corollary
3.27
, we conclude that

\varphi((1/2)P+P^{\perp})=(1/2)\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}.

Now,

P^{\perp}\sim 0,I,(1/2)P+P^{\perp}

yields

\varphi(P^{\perp})\sim 0,I,(1/2)\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}.

Since
\ell_{I,(1/2)\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}}=\{t\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}\,:\,t\in{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}\}
, we get

\varphi(P^{\perp})=\varphi(P)^{\perp}.

Using the same arguments as above, we see that

\varphi((1/2)P^{\perp})=(1/2)\varphi(P)^{\perp}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \varphi(P+(1/2)P^{\perp})=\varphi(P)+(1/2)\varphi(P)^{\perp}.

∎

Claim 4.3
.

Equation (
4.2
) holds for any
a,b\in\{0,1/3,1\}
.

Proof.

Take
Q\in\mathcal{P}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(PQ)=1/2
.
Claim
4.2
implies that

\varphi((1/2)Q)=(1/2)\varphi(Q),\ \ \varphi((1/2)Q^{\perp})=(1/2)\varphi(Q)^{\perp}.

By Lemma
3.43
, we have

P+(1/3)P^{\perp}\in\mathcal{C}_{(1/2)Q}\cap\mathcal{C}_{(1/2)Q^{\perp}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{I}.

Thus we obtain

(4.3)

\begin{split}\varphi(P+(1/3)P^{\perp})&\in\mathcal{C}_{(1/2)\varphi(Q)}\cap\mathcal{C}_{(1/2)\varphi(Q)^{\perp}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{I}\\
&=\{R+(1/3)R^{\perp}\,:\,R\in\mathcal{P},\,\mathrm{tr}\,(\varphi(Q)R)=1/2\}.\end{split}

Because
P+(1/3)P^{\perp}\in\ell_{P,I}
, we have

(4.4)

\varphi(P+(1/3)P^{\perp})\in\ell_{\varphi(P),\varphi(I)}=\ell_{\varphi(P),I}=\{\varphi(P)+a\varphi(P)^{\perp}\,:\,a\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\}.

It follows from (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) that

\varphi(P+(1/3)P^{\perp})=\varphi(P)+(1/3)\varphi(P)^{\perp},

and similarly,

\varphi(P^{\perp}+(1/3)P)=\varphi(P)^{\perp}+(1/3)\varphi(P).

Further,
\varphi((1/3)P)\in\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}

and
\varphi((1/3)P)\sim\varphi(P)^{\perp}+(1/3)\varphi(P)
.
It follows from Lemma
3.25
that

\varphi((1/3)P)=(1/3)\varphi(P)\ \ \ \text{and similarly,}\ \ \ \varphi((1/3)P^{\perp})=(1/3)\varphi(P)^{\perp}.

Finally,
\varphi((1/3)I)
belongs to the lines

\ell_{(1/3)\varphi(P),(1/3)\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}}\not=\ell_{(1/3)\varphi(P)^{\perp},(1/3)\varphi(P)^{\perp}+\varphi(P)}

which intersect in the point
(1/3)I
. Thus,

\varphi((1/3)I)=(1/3)I
.
∎

In what follows, we sometimes make use of the coherency preserving map
\varphi_{1}\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined by
\varphi_{1}(X)=I-\varphi(I-X)
,
X\in[0,I]
. Clearly,

\varphi_{1}(0)=0,\ \ \varphi_{1}((1/2)I)=(1/2)I,\ \ \varphi_{1}(I)=I,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi_{1}(P)=\varphi(P).

Claim 4.4
.

Equation (
4.2
) holds for any
a,b\in\{0,1/3,2/3,1\}
.

Proof.

Applying Claim
4.3
to the mapping
\varphi_{1}
in place of
\varphi
, we have (
4.2
) for every
a,b\in\{0,2/3,1\}
.

Let
a,b\in\{0,1/3,2/3,1\}
.
We have shown that (
4.2
) holds when one of the numbers
a,b
equals
0
or
1
.
In the remaining cases, we have
\{aP+bP^{\perp}\}=\ell_{aP,aP+P^{\perp}}\cap\ell_{bP^{\perp},P+bP^{\perp}}
. Thus

\begin{split}\varphi(aP+bP^{\perp})&\in\ell_{\varphi(aP),\varphi(aP+P^{\perp})}\cap\ell_{\varphi(bP^{\perp}),\varphi(P+bP^{\perp})}\\
&=\ell_{a\varphi(P),a\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}}\cap\ell_{b\varphi(P)^{\perp},\varphi(P)+b\varphi(P)^{\perp}}\\
&=\{a\varphi(P)+b\varphi(P)^{\perp}\},\end{split}

and consequently,
\varphi(aP+bP^{\perp})=a\varphi(P)+b\varphi(P)^{\perp}
.
∎

Claim 4.5
.

For every positive integer
n
, equation (
4.2
) holds for any

a,b\in\left\{0,{1\over 3}\left({2\over 3}\right)^{n-1},\left({2\over 3}\right)^{n}\right\}.

Proof.

The case
n=1
is true by the previous claim. So, assume that the above is true for some positive integer
n
.
We introduce a new coherency preserving map
\tau\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\tau(X)=\left(3/2\right)^{n}\varphi\left(\left(2/3\right)^{n}X\right),\ \ \ X\in[0,I].

Using the induction hypothesis, we see that

\tau(0)=0,\ \ \ \tau((1/2)I)=(1/2)I,\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \tau(I)=I.

Moreover,
\tau(P)=\varphi(P)
.
But then we already know that

\tau(a^{\prime}P+b^{\prime}P^{\perp})=a^{\prime}\varphi(P)+b^{\prime}\varphi(P)^{\perp}

for any
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\{0,1/3,2/3\}
. This immediately implies that (
4.2
) holds for any

a,b\in\left\{0,{1\over 3}\left({2\over 3}\right)^{n},\left({2\over 3}\right)^{n+1}\right\},

as desired.
∎

Claim 4.6
.

For every positive integer
n
, we have

(4.5)

\varphi\left(\left(1-\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{n}\right)P\right)=\left(1-\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{n}\right)\varphi(P)

and

(4.6)

\varphi\left(P+\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{n}P^{\perp}\right)=\varphi(P)+\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{n}\varphi(P)^{\perp}.

Proof.

We know that

(4.7)

\varphi((2/3)^{n}P)=(2/3)^{n}\varphi(P)\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \varphi((2/3)^{n}P^{\perp})=(2/3)^{n}\varphi(P)^{\perp}

for every positive integer
n
.

Using (
4.7
) for the coherency preserving map
\varphi_{1}
, we arrive at

\varphi(P+(1-(2/3)^{n})P^{\perp})=\varphi(P)+(1-(2/3)^{n})\varphi(P)^{\perp}

and

\varphi((1-(2/3)^{n})P+P^{\perp})=(1-(2/3)^{n})\varphi(P)+\varphi(P)^{\perp}

for every positive integer
n
.

We now use the fact that

\varphi((1-(2/3)^{n})P)\sim 0,\,\varphi(P),\,\varphi((1-(2/3)^{n})P+P^{\perp})

combined with Lemma
3.25
to deduce that

\varphi((1-(2/3)^{n})P)=(1-(2/3)^{n})\varphi(P)

and similarly,

\varphi((1-(2/3)^{n})P^{\perp})=(1-(2/3)^{n})\varphi(P)^{\perp}

for every positive integer
n
.

The last equation remains true if we replace
\varphi
by
\varphi_{1}
. This yields

\varphi(P+(2/3)^{n}P^{\perp})=\varphi(P)+(2/3)^{n}\varphi(P)^{\perp}

for every positive integer
n
.
∎

Claim 4.7
.

There is a
2\times 2
unitary matrix
U\in U_{2}
such that

\text{either}\ \ \varphi(P)=UPU^{\ast},\ \ P\in\mathcal{P},\ \ \text{or}\ \ \varphi(P)=UP^{t}U^{\ast},\ \ P\in\mathcal{P}.

Proof.

Let
a
be a positive real number
<1/2
. Using Lemmas
3.13
and
3.14
, we see that a pair
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}
satisfies

([0,I]\supset)\,\,\mathcal{S}_{(1-a)P,P+aP^{\perp}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{(1-a)Q,Q+aQ^{\perp}}\not=\emptyset

if and only if

\|P-Q\|\leq{a\over 1-a}.

Therefore, the two equations (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) imply that for any positive integer
n>2
, we have

\|P-Q\|\leq{(2/3)^{n}\over 1-(2/3)^{n}}\Rightarrow\|\varphi(P)-\varphi(Q)\|\leq{(2/3)^{n}\over 1-(2/3)^{n}}.

Applying (
3.9
), we conclude that there exists a sequence of positive real numbers
a_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, such that
\lim a_{n}=0
and

d^{g}(P,Q)\leq a_{n}\Rightarrow d^{g}(\varphi(P),\varphi(Q))\leq a_{n}

for every positive integer
n
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}
,
P\not=Q
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Take a positive integer
n
such that
a_{n}<\varepsilon
. Find a positive integer
k
such that

d^{g}(P,Q)\leq ka_{n}<d^{g}(P,Q)+\varepsilon.

By Lemma
3.8
, we can find rank one projections
P_{0}=P,P_{1},\ldots,P_{k-1},P_{k}=Q
such that
d^{g}(P_{j-1},P_{j})\leq a_{n}
. But then

\begin{split}d^{g}(\varphi(P),\varphi(Q))&\leq d^{g}(\varphi(P_{0}),\varphi(P_{1}))+\cdots+d^{g}(\varphi(P_{k-1}),\varphi(P_{k}))\\
&\leq ka_{n}<d^{g}(P,Q)+\varepsilon.\end{split}

Therefore,

d^{g}(\varphi(P),\varphi(Q))\leq d^{g}(P,Q),\ \ \ P,Q\in\mathcal{P}.

Applying (
3.9
) once more, we conclude that

\|\varphi(P)-\varphi(Q)\|\leq\|P-Q\|,\ \ \ P,Q\in\mathcal{P}.

Finally, it follows from Lemma
3.11
and
\varphi(P^{\perp})=\varphi(P)^{\perp}
that

\|\varphi(P)-\varphi(Q)\|=\|P-Q\|,\ \ \ P,Q\in\mathcal{P}.

Now, Wigner’s theorem (Theorem
3.9
, see also Remark
3.10
) completes the proof.
∎

Hence, after composing
\varphi
with the standard automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
given by
X\mapsto U^{\ast}XU
,
and possibly with the standard automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
given by
X\mapsto X^{t}
, we get to the situation where

\varphi(P)=P,\ \ \ P\in\mathcal{P}.

The following claim finally completes the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Claim 4.8
.

In the above situation, we have
\varphi(A)=A
for every
A\in[0,I]
.

Proof.

Fix
P\in\mathcal{P}
and a real number
t
,
1/3\leq t\leq 1
.
By
0\sim tP\sim P
,
\varphi(0)=0
, and
\varphi(P)=P
, there exists
s\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
such that
\varphi(tP)=sP
.
It follows from Claim
4.3
that for every
Q\in\mathcal{P}
we have

\varphi((1/3)Q+Q^{\perp})=(1/3)Q+Q^{\perp}.

Further,

(1/3)Q+Q^{\perp}-tP=((1/3)Q+Q^{\perp})\,(I-(3Q+Q^{\perp})(tP)).

Therefore, by Lemma
2.2
, the condition
(1/3)Q+Q^{\perp}\sim tP
holds if and only if

1=\mathrm{tr}\,((3Q+Q^{\perp})(tP))=\mathrm{tr}\,((2Q+I)(tP))=t(1+2\mathrm{tr}\,(QP)).

Because
(1-t)/(2t)\in[0,1]
, we can find a projection
Q\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathrm{tr}\,(QP)=(1-t)/(2t)
, and then by the above,

(1/3)Q+Q^{\perp}\sim tP
. It follows that
(1/3)Q+Q^{\perp}\sim sP
, which combined with Lemma
3.25
yields that
t=s
.

Hence, for every real
t
,
1/3\leq t\leq 1
, and every
P\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
\varphi(tP)=tP
.
Using the coherency preserving map
\varphi_{1}\colon X\mapsto I-\varphi(I-X)
, we obtain
\varphi(tP+P^{\perp})=tP+P^{\perp}
for every real
t
,
0\leq t\leq 2/3
, and every
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
Since
\varphi(tP)\sim 0,\varphi(P),\varphi(tP+P^{\perp})
, we get
\varphi(tP)=tP
when
0\leq t\leq 2/3
by Lemma
3.25
.
We conclude that

\varphi(tP)=tP,\ \ \ P\in\mathcal{P},\ \,t\in[0,1].

Let now
A
be any element of
[0,I]
of rank two. Applying Lemma
3.58
with
B=\varphi(A)
, we see that
\varphi(A)=A
or
\varphi(A)=0
.

We have shown that
\varphi(A)\in\{0,A\}
holds for every
A\in[0,I]
.
We now use the obtained result for the map
\varphi_{1}\colon X\mapsto I-\varphi(I-X)
to conclude that
\varphi(A)\in\{A,I\}
for every
A\in[0,I]
. Hence we obtain
\varphi(A)=A
for every
A\in[0,I]
.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
\varphi:[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver with the property that
\varphi(0)
,
\varphi((1/2)I)
, and
\varphi(I)
are in timelike position. Then
\varphi
is standard.

Proof.

This statement is a straightforward consequence of Proposition
4.1
and Example
3.40
.
∎

Proof of Theorem A.

Suppose that
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies the assumption of Theorem A.
By Corollary
3.21
, there is a standard automorphism
\psi_{1}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying

\psi_{1}([0,I])=[A,B],\ \ \psi_{1}(0)=A,\ \ \psi_{1}(I)=B,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \psi_{1}((1/2)I)=C.

It follows that the restriction of the coherency preserver
\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
to
[0,I]
satisfies the assumption of Corollary
4.9
.
Thus, Corollary
4.9
and Theorem
3.55
imply that
\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
is standard, which further shows that
\varphi
is standard, too.
∎

4.2.
Applications of Theorem A

We begin with a useful lemma.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open connected subset of
H_{2}
.
For any pair
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}
, there are
X_{1}=X,X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}=Y\in\mathcal{U}
such that
X_{1}\sim X_{2}\sim\cdots\sim X_{n}
.

Proof.

For
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}
, we write
X\approx Y
if there are
X_{1}=X,X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}=Y\in\mathcal{U}
such that
X_{1}\sim X_{2}\sim\cdots\sim X_{n}
.
It is clear that
\approx
is an equivalence relation.

First, let us consider the case where
\mathcal{U}
is an open interval.
When
X<Y
, then we have
\mathcal{S}_{X,Y}\subset\mathcal{U}
by Corollary
3.19
. We may take any point
Z\in\mathcal{S}_{X,Y}\subset\mathcal{U}
, and then
X\sim Z\sim Y
. Thus
X\approx Y
.
If
X,Y
are arbitrary, then we may take
Z\in\mathcal{U}
such that
X<Z
,
Y<Z
, hence we obtain
X\approx Z\approx Y
.
Thus the proof is complete when
\mathcal{U}
is an open interval.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an arbitrary open connected subset of
H_{2}
.
Fix
X_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
, and let
\mathcal{U}_{0}
denote the collection of
Y\in\mathcal{U}
with
X_{0}\approx Y
.
Then the preceding paragraph shows that
\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
\mathcal{U}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{0}
are open.
Since
\mathcal{U}
is connected, we obtain
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\mathcal{U}
.
∎

Let us prove the fundamental theorem of chronogeometry as a consequence of the preceding subsection.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to H_{2}
be a bijective map satisfying

A\sim B\iff\varphi(A)\sim\varphi(B)

for every pair
A,B\in H_{2}
. Then
\varphi
extends to an affine automorphism.

Proof.

After composing
\varphi
with a translation, we can assume with no loss of generality that
\varphi(0)=0
.
As before, we define
d(A,B)=\mathrm{rank}\,(A-B)\in\{0,1,2\}
,
A,B\in H_{2}
. It is clear that we have
d(A,B)=d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))
,
A,B\in H_{2}
.
The set
H_{2}^{2}
of invertible matrices in
H_{2}
is formed of
A\in H_{2}
with the property
d(A,0)=2
. Thus we get

\varphi(H_{2}^{2})=H_{2}^{2}.

Recall that
H_{2}^{2}
is the disjoint union of three sets
H_{2}^{++},H_{2}^{--}
, and
H_{2}^{+-}
.
We first claim that

\varphi(H_{2}^{++}\cup H_{2}^{--})=H_{2}^{++}\cup H_{2}^{--}.

The above equality follows directly from the following characterization of the set
H_{2}^{++}\cup H_{2}^{--}
: An invertible matrix
A\in H_{2}
belongs to
H_{2}^{++}\cup H_{2}^{--}
if and only if for each line
\ell
passing through zero there exists
B\in\ell\cap H_{2}
such that
A\sim B
.
Indeed, let
A\in H_{2}^{++}
and
\ell=\{aP\,:\,a\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\}
where
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Then
A^{-1}>0
and therefore
\mathrm{tr}\,(A^{-1}P)=\mathrm{tr}\,(PA^{-1}P)>0
. It follows that there exists exactly one (positive)
a\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathrm{tr}\,(A^{-1}(aP))=1
, or equivalently,
A\sim aP
(see Lemma
2.2
).
In the same way we treat the case when
A\in H_{2}^{--}
. So, assume finally that
A\in H_{2}^{+-}
. Then
A^{-1}
is again in
H_{2}^{+-}
.
Therefore, there exists a unit vector
x\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that
x^{\ast}A^{-1}x=0
, yielding that
\mathrm{tr}\,(A^{-1}Q)=0
, where
Q=xx^{\ast}
.
It follows from Lemma
2.2
that there is no point in
\{aQ\,:\,a\in\mathbb{R}\}
that is coherent to
A
.

Observe that if
A,B\in H_{2}^{++}\cup H_{2}^{--}
and
A\sim B
, then either
A,B\in H_{2}^{++}
, or
A,B\in H_{2}^{--}
.
Moreover,
H_{2}^{++}
and
H_{2}^{--}
are open and connected sets.
Thus Lemma
4.10
implies that

\varphi(H_{2}^{++})=H_{2}^{++}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \varphi(H_{2}^{--})=H_{2}^{--}\,;

or

\varphi(H_{2}^{++})=H_{2}^{--}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \varphi(H_{2}^{--})=H_{2}^{++}\,.

Therefore, we get
\varphi(-I)<0=\varphi(0)<\varphi(I)
or
\varphi(-I)>0=\varphi(0)>\varphi(I)
.
Hence Corollary
3.45
implies that the triple
\varphi(-I),\varphi(0),\varphi(I)
is in timelike position.
Theorem A shows that
\varphi
extends to an automorphism
\hat{\varphi}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Note that
\hat{\varphi}(H_{2})=H_{2}
implies
\hat{\varphi}({\widehat{\infty}})={\widehat{\infty}}
.
It remains to show that
\hat{\varphi}
is an affine automorphism.
By composing
\hat{\varphi}
with an appropriate affine automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
, we may assume
\hat{\varphi}(0)=0
and
\hat{\varphi}(I)=I
without loss of generality. (Recall that the composition of affine automorphisms is again an affine automorphism by Lemma
3.7
.)

Then the automorphism
\psi\colon X\mapsto X^{-1}-I
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies

\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}\circ\psi(0)=\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}({\widehat{\infty}})=\psi^{-1}({\widehat{\infty}})=0,

\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}\circ\psi(I)=\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}(0)=\psi^{-1}(0)=I,\ \ \text{and}

\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}\circ\psi((1/2)I)=\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}(I)=\psi^{-1}(I)=(1/2)I.

Now we apply Proposition
4.1
.
We see that there is a
2\times 2
unitary matrix
U
such that
\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}\circ\psi(X)=UXU^{*}
holds for every
X\in[0,I]
, or
\psi^{-1}\circ\hat{\varphi}\circ\psi(X)=UX^{t}U^{*}
holds for every
X\in[0,I]
.
By the identity-type theorem (Theorem
3.55
), the same equality holds for every
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
It is easy to see that
\psi(UXU^{*})=U\psi(X)U^{*}
and
\psi(X^{t})=\psi(X)^{t}
for every
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
It follows that
\hat{\varphi}(X)=UXU^{*}
holds for every
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
, or
\hat{\varphi}(X)=UX^{t}U^{*}
holds for every
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Assume that
\phi\colon{\mathcal{M}}_{4}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{4}
is a bijection that preserves lightlikeness in both directions.
We need to show that
\phi
is of the form (
1.3
).
We may assume
\phi(0)=0
without loss of generality.
Let
\xi\colon\mathcal{M}_{4}\to H_{2}
be the mapping as in (
1.4
).
Then the mapping
\varphi:=\xi\circ\phi\circ\xi^{-1}\colon H_{2}\to H_{2}
clearly satisfies the assumption of Theorem
4.11
.
It follows that
\varphi
extends to an affine automorphism.
In particular, we see that
\varphi
is linear.
It follows that
\phi=\xi^{-1}\circ\varphi\circ\xi
is also linear, that is,
\phi(r)=Sr
for some real
4\times 4
matrix
S
. Our task is to show that
S=cQ
for some positive real number
c
and some Lorentz matrix
Q
.
We know that for every
r\in\mathcal{M}_{4}
we have

\langle r,r\rangle=0\iff\langle Sr,Sr\rangle=0,

that is,

(Mr,r)=0\iff(S^{t}MSr,r)=0.

Here,
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the standard inner product on the space of all real quadruples, and
M
is as in (
1.1
). Choosing suitable spacetime events
r
(say,
r=(\cos\gamma,\sin\gamma,0,1),\ldots
) and applying the fact that
S^{t}MS
is symmetric, it is trivial to deduce that

S^{t}MS=dM

for some real number
d
. The matrix
S^{t}MS
has the same inertia as
M
, that is, it has three negative eigenvalues and one positive eigenvalue. It follows that
d
is positive, and consequently,

\left({1\over\sqrt{d}}S\right)^{t}\,M\,\left({1\over\sqrt{d}}S\right)=M,

as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

Every automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is standard.
An automorphism
\varphi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is an affine automorphism if and only if
\varphi({{\widehat{\infty}}})={{\widehat{\infty}}}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be an automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Since the triple
0,I,(1/2)I
is timelike (Example
3.40
), we see that the triple
\varphi(0),\varphi(I),\varphi((1/2)I)
is also timelike.
Thus Theorem A together with Theorem
3.55
implies that
\varphi
is standard.
If in addition
\varphi({\widehat{\infty}})={\widehat{\infty}}
, then we get
\varphi(H_{2})=H_{2}
.
Thus Theorems
4.11
and
3.55
show that
\varphi
is an affine automorphism.
Conversely, it is clear that an affine automorphism
\varphi
satisfies
\varphi({{\widehat{\infty}}})={{\widehat{\infty}}}
.
∎

Therefore, we no longer need to make the distinction between an automorphism and a standard automorphism.

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
.
An automorphism
\varphi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies
\varphi(A)=B
if and only if there is an affine automorphism
\psi
satisfying

\varphi(X)=\psi((X-A)^{-1})^{-1}+B

for every
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
.

Proof.

If
\psi
is an affine automorphism, then

\psi((A-A)^{-1})^{-1}+B=\psi({\widehat{\infty}})^{-1}+B={\widehat{\infty}}^{-1}+B=B.

Conversely, assume that an automorphism
\varphi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies
\varphi(A)=B
.
Define two automorphisms
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
by
\varphi_{1}(X):=X^{-1}+A
,
\varphi_{2}(X):=(X-B)^{-1}
,
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Then
\psi:=\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\varphi_{1}
is an automorphism.
By
\varphi(A)=B
, we get
\psi({\widehat{\infty}})={\widehat{\infty}}
, or equivalently,
\psi
is an affine automorphism.
We get

\varphi(X)=\varphi_{2}^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\varphi_{1}^{-1}(X)=\psi((X-A)^{-1})^{-1}+B

for every
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
, as desired.
∎

In particular, if
A=B=0
, then
\varphi
is of the form
\varphi(X)=\psi(X^{-1})^{-1}
.
We may also see that a general automorphism can be expressed as the composition of only a few affine automorphisms and inversions.
To show this, let
\varphi
be an automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
From the fact that the cone with vertex
{\widehat{\infty}}
is mapped by
\varphi
onto some cone, it is easy to see that there is
A\in H_{2}
such that
B:=\varphi(A)\in H_{2}
.
Thus the preceding corollary shows that
\varphi
is expressed as the composition of two inversions and three affine automorphisms (two of which are translations).

5.
Degenerate coherency preservers

5.1.
Two types of degenerate coherency preservers

We first give some examples of coherency preservers that are not standard.
This is to motivate the definition of degenerate coherency preservers.
Those who want to get to the main result immediately may look at Definitions
5.6
,
5.8
,
5.9
, and Theorem B, then skip to Subsection
5.2
.

For
n\in\{0,1,2\}
, let
H_{2}^{n}
denote the set of matrices in
H_{2}
of rank
n
.
Observe that
H_{2}^{2}
is the set of all invertible (
=
nonsingular) matrices in
H_{2}
.

Example 5.1
.

Obviously, any mapping from
\mathcal{A}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
into a line
\ell
is a coherency preserver.
As a special case, let
P
be any projection of rank one, and define
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\varphi(A)=(\mathrm{tr}\,A)P,\ \ \ A\in H_{2}.

This is a coherency preserver, and it is of interest to observe that
\varphi
is linear.

Example 5.2
.

Another simple example is the map
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined by

\varphi(A)=A,\ \ A\in H_{2}\setminus H_{2}^{2}

and

(5.1)

\varphi(A)=0,\ \ A\in H_{2}^{2}.

A slight modification gives a much “wilder” example. We choose any function
\eta\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
and for every rank one projection
P

we choose a function
f_{P}\colon\mathbb{R}\to{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
satisfying
f_{P}(0)=0
. Define
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\varphi(tP)=f_{P}(t)\eta(P),\ \ (t,P)\in\mathbb{R}\times{\mathcal{P}}

and (
5.1
).
It is easily seen from Lemma
3.22
that any such
\varphi
preserves coherency.
If
\eta
and all functions
f_{P}
are chosen to be surjective, then the image of
\varphi
is the whole cone
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Observe that the range of a coherency preserver in the above example is contained in one cone.
We will give the general form of such coherency preservers in Proposition
5.21
.
After some more reflection, one may encounter an example of a coherency preserver whose range slightly protrudes one cone.
Here is such an example:

Example 5.3
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
be any mapping, and define
\varphi\colon{\overline{H_{2}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\varphi(0)=0,\ \ \varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{0})=\{I\},

and

\varphi(tP)=f(P),\ \ P\in{\mathcal{P}},\ \ t\in{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}\setminus\{0\}.

It is easy and left to the reader to show that
\varphi
is a coherency preserver.
Observe that
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}})=f({\mathcal{P}})\cup\{0,I\}
is not necessarily contained in one cone.

Note that
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\{0\})=f({\mathcal{P}})\cup\{I\}
is contained in the cone
\mathcal{C}_{I}
, in this example.
Let us give another example, which is seemingly more complicated.

Example 5.4
.

We denote
by
\mathcal{L}\subset H_{2}^{1}
the set of all rank one matrices that are of the form

t\,\left[\begin{matrix}s&i\sqrt{s(1-s)}\cr-i\sqrt{s(1-s)}&1-s\end{matrix}\right]

for some nonzero real
t
and some real
s
,
0\leq s\leq 1
.
We set

\mathcal{K}=\left\{\left[\begin{matrix}0&p\cr p&0\end{matrix}\right]\ :\,p\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\right\}\subset H_{2}^{2}.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\to{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
and
\eta\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{P}
be any maps.
We define
\varphi_{1}\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\varphi_{1}(0)=0,\ \ \varphi_{1}(H_{2}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{L})=\{E_{11}\},\ \ \varphi_{1}(H_{2}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{K})=\{I\},

\varphi_{1}\left(t\,\left[\begin{matrix}s&i\sqrt{s(1-s)}\cr-i\sqrt{s(1-s)}&1-s\end{matrix}\right]\right)=\eta(s),\ \ (t,s)\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times[0,1],

and

\varphi_{1}\left(\left[\begin{matrix}0&p\cr p&0\end{matrix}\right]\right)=g(p)E_{22}+E_{11},\ \ p\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

Then
\varphi_{1}
is a coherency preserver.

Proof.

Assume that
A,B\in H_{2}
are coherent. Observe that rank one matrices are mapped to rank one matrices. Thus, if one of
A
or
B
is the zero matrix, then the other one is of rank at most one, and therefore
\varphi_{1}(A)\sim\varphi_{1}(B)
. The next case we will treat is the one where both
A
and
B
are of rank one, that is,
A=tP
and
B=sP
for some rank one projection
P
and some nonzero scalars
t,s
.
Then clearly,
\varphi_{1}(A)=\varphi_{1}(B)
. We continue by treating the case where both
A
and
B
are invertible. Using the fact that
\varphi_{1}
maps the set of invertible matrices into the line

\ell=\left\{tE_{22}+E_{11}\,:\,t\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\right\},

we see that
\varphi_{1}(A)\sim\varphi_{1}(B)
in this case, as well.

It remains to consider the case when one of
A,B
, say
A
, is of rank one, and
B
is of rank two. Then clearly,
\varphi_{1}(A)\in\mathcal{P}
.
If
B\not\in\mathcal{K}
, then
\varphi_{1}(B)=I
and
\varphi_{1}(A)\sim\varphi_{1}(B)
. If
B\in\mathcal{K}
, then Lemma
5.5
below implies
A\not\in\mathcal{L}
, which yields

\varphi_{1}(A)=E_{11}
. Therefore, we have
\varphi_{1}(A)\sim\varphi_{1}(B)
in this last case, too.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
p,t
be nonzero real numbers and let
s
be a real number,
0\leq s\leq 1
. Then the matrices

A=t\,\left[\begin{matrix}s&i\sqrt{s(1-s)}\cr-i\sqrt{s(1-s)}&1-s\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathcal{L}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ B=\left[\begin{matrix}0&p\cr p&0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathcal{K}

are not coherent.

Proof.

Observe that
A
is of rank one while
B
is invertible. We have

B-A=B(I-B^{-1}A)
.
It is straightforward to verify that
B^{-1}A
is a trace zero complex matrix and since it is of rank one, it is a square-zero matrix. Consequently,
I-B^{-1}A
is invertible, which further yields that
B-A
is of rank two, as desired.
∎

For
\varphi_{1}
in Example
5.4
, we see that the image
\varphi_{1}(H_{2}\setminus\{0\})
is contained in the cone
\mathcal{C}_{I}
.
Thus, for each of the above examples of coherency preservers
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
, there are
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
B\in\varphi(\mathcal{A})
with the property
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{A\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{B}
.
We give a name to this class of coherency preservers.

Definition 5.6
.

A coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined on a subset
\mathcal{A}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is of
type
(\mathcal{C})
if there exist
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\varphi(\mathcal{A})
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{A\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{B}
.

Is every non-standard coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
? The answer is no.

Example 5.7
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
be any function.
Let us define a map
\varphi_{2}\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
in the following way.
We start by defining

\varphi_{2}(tE_{11})=f(t)E_{11}

for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
If a matrix
A\in H_{2}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}}
is coherent to
tE_{11}
for some real number
t
, then such a
t
is uniquely determined.
In this case, we set

\varphi_{2}(A)=E_{22}+f(t)E_{11}.

In the remaining case where
d(A,tE_{11})=2
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
(equivalently,
A
is of rank two and the
(2,2)
-entry of
A
is zero), we define

\varphi_{2}(A)=E_{22}.

Then
\varphi_{2}
is a coherency preserving map.

Proof.

We need to show that
\varphi_{2}(A)\not\sim\varphi_{2}(B)
implies
A\not\sim B
.
Observe that
\varphi(H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{0,E_{11}}
and
\varphi(H_{2}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{E_{22},I}
.
Therefore,
\varphi_{2}(A)\not\sim\varphi_{2}(B)
implies that one of the two matrices
A,B
is in
H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}}
and the other is in
H_{2}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}}
.
In that case, it is obvious from the definition of
\varphi_{2}
that
\varphi_{2}(A)\not\sim\varphi_{2}(B)
implies
A\not\sim B
.
∎

Observe that
\varphi_{2}
defined in this manner is not necessarily of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
However, it is a coherency preserver of type
(\ell)
in the following sense.

Definition 5.8
.

A coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined on a subset
\mathcal{A}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is of
type
(\ell)

if there are lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\ell)\subset\ell^{\prime}
.

Note that the two types
(\mathcal{C})
,
(\ell)
of coherency preservers are not disjoint.
For example, any mapping
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
whose range is contained in a single line is a coherency preserver of both types
(\mathcal{C})
and
(\ell)
.
Note also that in both types the range of
\varphi
is contained in a “small” subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Indeed, if
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
, then its range is contained in a union of a point and a cone; and if
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
, then its range is contained in a union of two lines.

It will turn out that the two types of coherency preservers play a dominant role in the class of non-standard coherency preservers, so let us give a name to the union of these two classes.

Definition 5.9
.

A coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined on a subset
\mathcal{A}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is
degenerate
if it is of type
(\mathcal{C})
or of type
(\ell)
.

Here we present the key result of the current section.

Theorem B
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
and
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver that is not standard. Then
\varphi
is degenerate.

We will give its lengthy proof in Section
6
.
Before that, we give applications of Theorem B in the next subsection.
It will turn out that a coherency preserver is either standard or degenerate if it is defined on a subset that looks like a matrix interval.

However, it is not true that a non-standard coherency preserver defined on, say, an open connected subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is always degenerate in the sense of Definition
5.9
.
We give some examples.
Let
\mathcal{U}\subset H_{2}
be the set formed of all matrices
X
in
H_{2}
such that the difference of the largest eigenvalue of
X
and the smallest eigenvalue is less than
1
. Clearly, it is open. It is also connected. Indeed, let
A,B\in\mathcal{U}
with eigenvalues
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
and
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
, respectively. It is trivial to find paths contained in
\mathcal{U}
from
A
to
((t_{1}+t_{2})/2)I
, from
((t_{1}+t_{2})/2)I
to
((s_{1}+s_{2})/2)I
, and from
((s_{1}+s_{2})/2)I
to
B
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subset H_{2}
be as above.
Assume that
A,B\in\mathcal{U}
are coherent. Then
|\mathrm{tr}\,(A-B)|<2
.

Proof.

We have
B=A+tP
for some real number
t
and some rank one projection
P
. Assume
t\geq 0
.
Then
\mathrm{tr}\,A\leq\mathrm{tr}\,B
. We need to verify that
t<2
. Let
p\leq q
be eigenvalues of
A
,
x\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
a unit vector spanning the image of
P
and
y
a unit vector orthogonal to
x
. We have

(Bx,x)=(Ax,x)+t(Px,x)\geq p+t

and

(By,y)=(Ay,y)+t(Py,y)\leq q

and therefore, the difference of the larger eigenvalue of
B
and the smaller eigenvalue of
B
is no smaller than
(p+t)-q=t-(q-p)>t-1
. It follows that
1>t-1
, as desired.
If
t\leq 0
, then exchange the roles of
A
and
B
.
∎

We first give an easy example.

Example 5.11
.

Let
a>3
be a real number.
Let
A_{n}\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, satisfy
A_{n}\sim A_{n+1}
for every
n
.
We define
\psi\colon\mathcal{U}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by
\psi(X)=A_{n}
for
X\in\mathcal{U}
with
an\leq\mathrm{tr}\,X<a(n+1)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since every pair
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}
with
X\sim Y
satisfies
|\mathrm{tr}\,(X-Y)|<2
, it is easy to see that
\psi
is a coherency preserver.

Next we give a more involved example that mixes coherency preservers of the two types
(\ell)
and
(\mathcal{C})
.

Example 5.12
.

Let
\varphi_{1}\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
given in Example
5.4
.
Let
\varphi_{2}\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver of type
(\ell)
given in Example
5.7
.
Let
\varphi_{3}\colon H_{2}\to\mathcal{C}_{0}
be any coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
Assume that
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
a>3
.
We define
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
by

\varphi(X)=\begin{cases}\varphi_{2}(X+aI)-E_{22},&\mathrm{tr}\,X<-a,\\
\varphi_{1}(X)-I,&-a\leq\mathrm{tr}\,X<a,\\
\varphi_{3}(X),&a\leq\mathrm{tr}\,X.\end{cases}

Then
\varphi
is a coherency preserver.

Proof.

Recall that
\varphi_{1}(X)=I
if
X>0
or
X<0
.
Thus we have
\varphi(X)=0
for
0<X\in\mathcal{U}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,X<a
, and for
0>X\in\mathcal{U}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,X\geq-a
.

Assume that
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}
are coherent.
We show that
\varphi(X)
is coherent to
\varphi(Y)
.
Since
Y-X
is of rank one, we see that
X\leq Y
or
Y\leq X
holds.
We may assume
X\leq Y
without loss of generality.
By Lemma
5.10
, we get
|\mathrm{tr}\,(X-Y)|<2
.
Obviously, one of the following holds:

•

The set
\{\mathrm{tr}\,X,\mathrm{tr}\,Y\}
is contained either in
(-\infty,-a)
,
[-a,a)
, or
[a,\infty)
.

•

\mathrm{tr}\,X<-a\leq\mathrm{tr}\,Y
.

•

\mathrm{tr}\,X<a\leq\mathrm{tr}\,Y
.

In the first case, it is clear that
\varphi(X)\sim\varphi(Y)
.

Assume
\mathrm{tr}\,X<-a\leq\mathrm{tr}\,Y
. Then we have
\mathrm{tr}\,Y<\mathrm{tr}\,X+2<-a+2<-1
. By the definition of
\mathcal{U}
, we see that
Y<0
, and we obtain
\varphi(Y)=\varphi_{1}(Y)-I=0
.
On the other hand, we know that
\mathrm{tr}\,X>\mathrm{tr}\,Y-2\geq-a-2
and thus
\mathrm{tr}\,(X+aI)>a-2>1
, which leads to
X+aI>0
. From the definition of
\varphi_{2}
, we have
\varphi(X)=\varphi_{2}(X+aI)-E_{22}\in\{sE_{11}\,:\,s\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\}
. Therefore,
\varphi(Y)=0\sim\varphi(X)
.

If
\mathrm{tr}\,X<a\leq\mathrm{tr}\,Y
, a similar argument shows that
X>0
and thus
\varphi(X)=\varphi_{1}(X)-I=0
. Since
\varphi(Y)=\varphi_{3}(Y)\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we have
\varphi(X)\sim\varphi(Y)
, as desired.
∎

Combining ideas in the above two examples together, one may construct various coherency preservers involving (possibly infinitely) many degenerate coherency preservers.
One may also construct similar examples of coherency preservers on, say, the open connected subset
\{X\in H_{2}\,:\,-1<\mathrm{tr}\,X<1\}\subset H_{2}
.

5.2.
Applications of Theorem B

In this subsection, we give several results assuming that Theorem B holds true.
The proof of Theorem B will be given later in Section
6
.
Recall that
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is endowed with the topology that comes from the identification with
U_{2}
as in Subsection
2.2
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
A_{n}\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
,
n\geq 1
, be a sequence that converges to
A
in the compact space
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Then

\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\mathcal{C}_{A_{n}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{A}.

Proof.

We may consider
U_{2}
instead of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Thus, let us think of
A_{n}
and
A
as elements in
U_{2}
.
The condition

B\in\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\mathcal{C}_{A_{n}}

means that
B\sim A_{n}
for infinitely many
n
.
Recall that
B\sim A_{n}
in
U_{2}
means that the rank of
B-A_{n}
is at most
1
.
Since
A_{n}\to A
, it follows that the rank of
B-A
is at most
1
, too.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\ell_{n}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
,
n\geq 1
, be a sequence of lines.
Then there is a subsequence
\ell_{n_{k}}
and a line
\ell
satisfying

\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{k\geq N}\ell_{n_{k}}\subset\ell.

Proof.

Take any
A_{n}\in\ell_{n}
for each
n
.
By passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
A_{n}
converges to some point
A\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
in the compact space
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
We may also assume
A=0
without loss of generality (use Lemma
3.47
).
Set
\mathcal{U}=\{X\in H_{2}\,:\,\|X\|<1\}
, which is an open neighborhood of
A=0
.
It is easy to see that we may take
B_{n}\in\ell_{n}\setminus\mathcal{U}
for each
n
.
By passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
B_{n}
converges to some element
B\in{\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{U}
.
Since
A_{n}\sim B_{n}
for each
n
, we have
A\sim B
(to see this, consider the corresponding points in
U_{2}
).
If

X\in\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\ell_{n},

then we have
A_{n}\sim X\sim B_{n}
for infinitely many
n
.
Since
A_{n}\to A
and
B_{n}\to B
, we have
X\in\ell_{A,B}
.
Thus we obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset H_{2}
be a subset and
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
a coherency preserver.
Assume that the following condition holds: For any pair
X,Y\in\mathcal{A}
, there are
X_{1}=X,X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}=Y\in\mathcal{A}
such that
X_{1}\sim X_{2}\sim\cdots\sim X_{n}
.
(By lemma
4.10
, this condition is satisfied whenever
\mathcal{A}
is open and connected.)
If
A\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies
\varphi(\mathcal{A})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{A}
, then either
A\in\varphi(\mathcal{A})
, or there is a line
\ell
passing through
A
with
\varphi(\mathcal{A})\subset\ell
.

Proof.

We may assume
A=0
without loss of generality.
The desired conclusion readily follows from Lemma
3.22
.
∎

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
with
A<B
. Any of the sets
(A,B)
,
\{C\in H_{2}\,:\,C>A\}
,
\{C\in H_{2}\,:\,C<A\}
, and
H_{2}
will be called an
open interval
in
H_{2}
.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open interval in
H_{2}
and
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
a coherency preserver. Then
\varphi
is either standard or degenerate.

Proof.

We may find
\{A_{n}\,:\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subset H_{2}
such that

\cdots<A_{-2}<A_{-1}<A_{0}<A_{1}<A_{2}<\cdots

and
\mathcal{U}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[A_{-n},A_{n}]
hold.
The restriction
\varphi_{n}\colon[A_{-n},A_{n}]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
of
\varphi
to
[A_{-n},A_{n}]
is obviously a coherency preserver.
Assume that
\varphi
is not standard.
Then Theorem
3.55
implies that for every
n\geq 1
the map
\varphi_{n}
is not standard.
It follows from Theorem B that
\varphi_{n}
is degenerate.

Assume that there are infinitely many
n\geq 1
such that
\varphi_{n}
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
By passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\varphi_{n}
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
for every
n\geq 1
.
For each
n\geq 1
, we may take
B_{n}\in[A_{-n},A_{n}]
and
C_{n}\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
such that
\varphi([A_{-n},A_{n}]\setminus\{B_{n}\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{C_{n}}
.
By passing to a subsequence again, we may assume that
\{B_{n}\}
and
\{C_{n}\}
converge in the compact space
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Let
B_{n}\to B
and
C_{n}\to C
as
n\to\infty
in
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
It follows that

\mathcal{U}\setminus\{B\}\subset\bigcup_{n\geq N}[A_{-n},A_{n}]\setminus\{B_{n}\}

for every
N\geq 1
. Therefore, we have

\varphi(\mathcal{U}\setminus\{B\})\subset\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\varphi([A_{-n},A_{n}]\setminus\{B_{n}\})\subset\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\mathcal{C}_{C_{n}}.

This together with Lemma
5.13
implies that
\varphi(\mathcal{U}\setminus\{B\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{C}
. Therefore,
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
if
C\in\varphi(\mathcal{U})
.
Assume that
C\notin\varphi(\mathcal{U})
. Since
\mathcal{U}\setminus\{B\}
is open and connected, Lemma
5.15
shows that
\varphi(\mathcal{U}\setminus\{B\})
is contained in one line. Thus
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Assume that there are infinitely many
n\geq 1
such that
\varphi_{n}
is of type
(\ell)
.
This case can be considered in a parallel manner as in the preceding case.
By passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\varphi_{n}
is of type
(\ell)
for every
n\geq 1
.
For each
n\geq 1
, we may take lines
\ell_{n}
,
\ell_{n}^{\prime}
such that
\varphi([A_{-n},A_{n}]\setminus\ell_{n})\subset\ell_{n}^{\prime}
.
By using Lemma
5.14
and passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\ell^{\prime}_{n}\subset\ell^{\prime}
for some line
\ell^{\prime}
.
Observe that the sequence
\bigcap_{n\geq N}\ell_{n}
,
N\geq 1
, of subsets of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
is expanding and that each of
\bigcap_{n\geq N}\ell_{n}
is either empty or a singleton or a line. Thus we see that
\bigcap_{n\geq N}\ell_{n}
is contained in a line
\ell
for all
N\geq 1
.
It follows that

\varphi(\mathcal{U}\setminus\ell)\subset\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\varphi([A_{-n},A_{n}]\setminus\ell_{n})\subset\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq N}\ell_{n}^{\prime}\subset\ell^{\prime}.

Thus
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.
∎

Almost the same proof shows that a coherency preserver defined on an interval of the form
[A,B)
, or
\{C\in H_{2}\,:\,C\geq A\}
, or
(A,B]
, or
\{C\in H_{2}\,:\,C\leq A\}
is also either standard of degenerate.
We also obtain the following.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\varphi\colon{\overline{H_{2}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver. Then
\varphi
is either standard or degenerate.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
, set
\mathcal{A}_{n}:=\{aP+bP^{\perp}\,:\,P\in{\mathcal{P}},\ a,b\in[-n,\infty]\}
.
Clearly, we have
\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{A}_{n}={\overline{H_{2}}}
.
If
\varphi
is not standard, then
\varphi
restricted to each
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is not standard, either.
Observe that the automorphism
X\mapsto(X+(n+1)I)^{-1}
sends
\mathcal{A}_{n}
onto
[0,I]
.
Therefore, Theorem B implies that
\varphi
restricted to each
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is degenerate.
Hence the same argument as above shows that
\varphi
is degenerate on
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
∎

Let us call a subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
a generalized open interval if it is an image of some open interval in
H_{2}
by an automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Observe that for every
X\in\mathcal{U}
there is a generalized open interval
\mathcal{I}
such that
X\in\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{U}
.
We say that a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is
locally degenerate
if
\varphi
is degenerate on every generalized open interval contained in
\mathcal{U}
.
Here is a consequence of Theorem
5.16
with the identity-type theorem (Theorem
3.55
).

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open connected subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Then every coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is either standard or locally degenerate.

Now, we are going to give a proof of Theorem
1.2
, which was the original goal of our research.
We need an easy lemma.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\ell\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a line and
\xi\colon\mathcal{M}_{4}\to H_{2}
the mapping as in (
1.4
).
Then
\xi^{-1}(H_{2}\cap\ell)
is contained in one lightlike line.

Proof.

There is nothing to prove when
\ell\cap H_{2}=\emptyset
.
If
\ell\cap H_{2}\neq\emptyset
, then Lemma
3.23
implies that there exist
A\in H_{2}
and
P\in\mathcal{P}
such that

\ell\cap H_{2}=\{A+aP\,:\,a\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
This can be rewritten as
\ell\cap H_{2}=\{B+t(2P)\,:\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
, where
B=A-(\mathrm{tr}\,A)P
is a trace zero matrix. The spacetime event
r_{0}
that corresponds to
B
via the identification (
1.4
) is of the form
r_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0},0)
. Applying the Bloch representation (Subsection
3.2
), we see that

\ell\cap H_{2}
corresponds to the lightlike line
\{(x_{0}+tx,y_{0}+ty,z_{0}+tz,t)\,:\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
for some
(x_{0},y_{0},z_{0}),(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Instead of working on
\mathcal{M}_{4}
, we may equivalently consider a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to H_{2}
.
By Theorem
5.16
,
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to H_{2}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
is either standard or degenerate.

If
\varphi
is standard, then
\varphi
extends to an automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
, which necessarily maps
{\widehat{\infty}}
to itself. By Corollary
4.12
, the extension is an affine automorphism, and the proof is complete in this case (see Subsection
4.2
).

Assume that
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
Then there are
A\in H_{2}
and
B\in H_{2}
such that
\varphi(H_{2}\setminus\{A\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{B}\cap H_{2}
. It immediately follows that the second item of Theorem
1.2
holds.

Assume that
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.
Then there are lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying
\varphi(H_{2}\setminus\ell)\subset H_{2}\cap\ell^{\prime}
.
It follows from Lemma
5.19
that the third item of Theorem
1.2
holds.
∎

A generalization of Alexandrov’s theorem given by Lester is also a simple consequence of our main result.

Theorem 5.20

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an open connected subset of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
If
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies

X\sim Y\iff\varphi(X)\sim\varphi(Y)

for every pair
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}
, then
\varphi
is standard.

Proof.

Theorem
5.18
tells us that
\varphi
is either standard or locally degenerate.
We need to show that
\varphi
is not locally degenerate.
By Lemma
3.18
, we just need to show that a degenerate coherency preserver
\varphi\colon(0,I)\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
never satisfies
X\sim Y\iff\varphi(X)\sim\varphi(Y)
for every pair
X,Y\in(0,I)
.

We prove it.
Assume that
\varphi\colon(0,I)\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is a degenerate coherency preserver satisfying
X\sim Y\iff\varphi(X)\sim\varphi(Y)
for every pair
X,Y\in(0,I)
. We need to obtain a contradiction.

If
\varphi
is of type
({\mathcal{C}})
, then there are
A\in(0,I)
and
B\in\varphi((0,I))
such that
\varphi((0,I)\setminus\{A\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{B}
.
Take
A<C<D<I
, Then we have
\mathcal{S}_{C,D}\subset[C,D]\subset(0,I)
by Corollary
3.19
.
Since
C\not\sim D
, we see that
\varphi(C)\not\sim\varphi(D)
.
This together with
\varphi(C),\varphi(D)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{B}
shows that
d(B,\varphi(C))=d(B,\varphi(D))=1
and
\ell_{B,\varphi(C)}\cap\ell_{B,\varphi(D)}=\{B\}
.
It follows that

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{C,D})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(C)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(D)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{B}=\ell_{B,\varphi(C)}\cap\ell_{B,\varphi(D)}=\{B\}.

However,
\mathcal{S}_{C,D}\,(\subset(0,I))
has infinitely many points.
For any
X\neq Y
in
\mathcal{S}_{C,D}
, we have
X\not\sim Y
, but
\varphi(X)=B=\varphi(Y)
, thus we obtain a contradiction.

If
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
, then there are lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
such that
\varphi((0,I)\setminus\ell)\subset\ell^{\prime}
.
It is clear that there is a pair
A,B\in(0,I)\setminus\ell
with
A\not\sim B
, but we have
\varphi(A),\varphi(B)\in\ell^{\prime}
, hence
\varphi(A)\sim\varphi(B)
, which contradicts our assumption.
∎

5.3.
More concrete description of degenerate coherency preservers

We would like to describe degenerate coherency preservers in a more concrete manner.
We remark that none of the results in this subsection depend on Theorem B.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a subset.
First, we study the special case where the image is contained in a cone.
Consider a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{A})
is contained in a cone.
By considering
\psi\circ\varphi
for an appropriate automorphism
\psi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
instead of
\varphi
, the description of such a map reduces to the case
\varphi(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}
without loss of generality.
We may give a complete characterization.

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}_{0}
be a mapping. For
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
we denote
\mathcal{A}_{P}:=\varphi^{-1}(\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})
. Then the following two conditions are equivalent.

•

The mapping
\varphi
is a coherency preserver.

•

A\not\sim B
holds for any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
P\neq Q
and any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{P}
,
B\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
.

Proof.

The mapping
\varphi
preserves coherency if and only if
\varphi(A)\not\sim\varphi(B)
implies
A\not\sim B
for every pair
A,B\in{\mathcal{A}}
.
By Lemma
3.22
, we see that
\varphi(A)\not\sim\varphi(B)
holds if and only if
A\in\mathcal{A}_{P}
and
B\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
hold for some
P,Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
P\neq Q
.
This leads to the desired conclusion.
∎

This generalizes Example
5.2
.
We may get more examples in the following manner.
Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}
be a subset satisfying
B_{1}\not\sim B_{2}
for any distinct
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}
, e.g.,
\mathcal{B}=\{tI\,:\,t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
,
\mathcal{B}=\{A\in H_{2}\,:\,\mathrm{tr}\,A=0\}
.
Take any mapping
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
satisfying
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{B})=\{0\}
.
Then
\varphi
satisfies the condition of Proposition
5.21
.

In what follows, we consider the case where the image is not contained in any single cone.
Let us begin with type
(\ell)
.
Consider a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
of type
(\ell)
.
We may take lines
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\ell_{1})\subset\ell_{2}^{\prime}
.
We may also take a line
\ell_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{1})\subset\ell_{1}^{\prime}
.
If
\varphi(\mathcal{A})
is not contained in any single cone, then we have
\ell_{1}^{\prime}\cap\ell_{2}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
By considering
\psi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
for an appropriate pair
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of automorphisms of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
instead of
\varphi
, the general description of such a map reduces to the case
\ell_{1}=\ell_{1}^{\prime}=\ell_{0,E_{11}}
and
\ell_{2}^{\prime}=\ell_{E_{22},I}
. (To see this, use Lemma
3.29
a few times.)

Proposition 5.22
.

A mapping
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
with
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{0,E_{11}}
and
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{E_{22},I}
is a coherency preserver if and only if the equation
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}_{A}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\{\varphi(A)+E_{22}\}
holds for every
A\in\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a mapping satisfying
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{0,E_{11}}
and
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{E_{22},I}
.
If
\varphi
preserves coherency, then we get
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}_{A}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\ell_{E_{22},I}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(A)}=\{\varphi(A)+E_{22}\}
for every
A\in\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}}
.

Conversely, assume that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}_{A}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}})\subset\{\varphi(A)+E_{22}\}
holds for every
A\in\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}}
.
If
A,B\in\mathcal{A}
satisfy
\varphi(A)\not\sim\varphi(B)
, then one of
A,B
lies in
\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,E_{11}}
and the other is in
\mathcal{A}\setminus\ell_{0,E_{11}}
.
Then it follows from the assumption that
A\not\sim B
, as desired.
∎

Observe that this generalizes Example
5.7
.

Lastly, we consider type
(\mathcal{C})
.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
We may take
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{A\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{B}
.
If
\varphi(\mathcal{A})
is not contained in any single cone, then we have
\varphi(A)\not\sim B
.
By considering
\psi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
for an appropriate pair
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of automorphisms of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
instead of
\varphi
, the general description of such a map reduces to the case
A=0
,
B=I
, and
\varphi(0)=0
.

Proposition 5.23
.

Assume that
0\in\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a mapping with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
.
For
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
we denote
\mathcal{A}_{P}:=\varphi^{-1}(\ell_{I,P}\setminus\{I\})
.
Then
\varphi
is a coherency preserver if and only if the following conditions hold.

•

A\not\sim B
holds for any
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}
with
P\not=Q
and any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{P}
,
B\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
.

•

For each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
there is
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})\subset\{Q\}
.

Proof.

If
\varphi
is a coherency preserver and
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, then
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})
is a coherent subset of
{\mathcal{P}}
, so there is
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{A}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})\subset\{Q\}
.
The rest of the proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
5.21
.
∎

In what follows, we observe that
\varphi
needs to be of a very special form in the case
\mathcal{A}=H_{2}
or
\mathcal{A}={\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Recall that
H_{2}^{2}=H_{2}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
is the collection of all invertible matrices in
H_{2}
.

Proposition 5.24
.

A mapping
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(H_{2}\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
is a coherency preserver if and only if one of the following conditions is satisfied.

(1)

\varphi(H_{2}^{2})=\{I\}
, and for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
there is
Q_{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q_{P}\}
.

(2)

There is a unique element
R\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\mathcal{B}=H_{2}^{2}\cap\varphi^{-1}(\ell_{I,R}\setminus\{I\})
is nonempty, and for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
there is
Q_{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q_{P}\}
. Moreover, for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
with the property that some point of
H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,P}
is coherent to some point of
\mathcal{B}
, we have
Q_{P}=R
.

Proof.

It is left to the reader to show that a mapping satisfying (1) or (2) preserves coherency.

Assume that
\varphi\colon H_{2}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(H_{2}\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
is a coherency preserver.
For each
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
\varphi(H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})
is a coherent subset of
{\mathcal{P}}
, so there is
Q_{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(H_{2}\cap\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q_{P}\}
.

Assume in addition (1) does not hold.
Then we may find
X\in H_{2}^{2}
satisfying
\varphi(X)=R+cR^{\perp}
with
R\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
c\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\setminus\{1\}
.
If
X>0
or
X<0
, then for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
there is
d\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
such that
X\sim dP
.
(Indeed, we know that
\mathcal{S}_{0,X}\subset H_{2}
by Lemma
3.19
, and that every line passing through
0
intersects
\mathcal{S}_{0,X}
.)
This implies
\varphi(dP)=R
, hence we obtain
Q_{P}=R
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Assume that
X\not>0
and
X\not<0
.
Then we may find
E\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that
X=c_{1}E-c_{2}E^{\perp}
.
By the lemma below, we have
Q_{P}=R
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(PE)\neq c_{1}/(c_{1}+c_{2})
.
In both cases, we have
Q_{P}=R
for all points
P
in the Bloch sphere possibly with the exception of points in one circle.
Thus we see that (2) holds in this case.
∎

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
E\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.
For each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(PE)\neq c_{1}/(c_{1}+c_{2})
, there is
d\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
such that
c_{1}E-c_{2}E^{\perp}\sim dP
.

Proof.

There is no loss of generality in assuming that
E=E_{11}
.
We may write

P=\left[\begin{matrix}c&e^{it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}\cr e^{-it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}&1-c\cr\end{matrix}\right]

for some
c\in[0,1]\setminus\{c_{1}/(c_{1}+c_{2})\}
and some
t\in[0,2\pi)
.
Then we have

c_{1}E-c_{2}E^{\perp}-dP=\left[\begin{matrix}c_{1}-dc&-de^{it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}\cr-de^{-it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}&-c_{2}-d(1-c)\cr\end{matrix}\right]

and thus

\det(c_{1}E-c_{2}E^{\perp}-dP)=(c_{1}-dc)(-c_{2}-d(1-c))-d^{2}(c-c^{2})=-c_{1}c_{2}+d((c_{1}+c_{2})c-c_{1}),

which takes the value
0
when
d=c_{1}c_{2}/((c_{1}+c_{2})c-c_{1})\neq 0
.
∎

Proposition 5.26
.

A mapping
\varphi\colon{\overline{H_{2}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
is a coherency preserver if and only if one of the following conditions holds.

•

There is
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{0}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q\}
and
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{0})\subset\ell_{Q,I}
.
(In this case, we have
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}
.)

•

\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{0})=\{I\}
, and for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
there is
Q_{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q_{P}\}
.

Proof.

If
\varphi\colon{\overline{H_{2}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
is a coherency preserver, then
\varphi(\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})
is a coherent subset of
{\mathcal{P}}
for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Thus there is
Q_{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi(\ell_{0,P}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q_{P}\}
.

Assume that
Q_{P}=Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Since every element of
{\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{0}
is coherent to some point of
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\setminus\{0\}
, we see that
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{0})\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}
. This together with the assumption implies
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{0})\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}\cap\mathcal{C}_{I}=\ell_{Q,I}
.

Assume that
Q_{P_{1}}\neq Q_{P_{2}}
for some pair
P_{1},P_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Observe that every element of
{\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
is coherent to some element of
\ell_{0,P_{1}}\setminus\{0\}
and some element of
\ell_{0,P_{2}}\setminus\{0\}
.
This together with

\varphi(\ell_{0,P_{1}}\setminus\{0\})=\{Q_{P_{1}}\}\neq\{Q_{P_{2}}\}=\varphi(\ell_{0,P_{1}}\setminus\{0\})

and
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
shows that
\varphi({\overline{H_{2}}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{0})=\{I\}
.

Thus we obtain the direct implication. The converse is easy to show.
∎

Observe that Proposition
5.24
generalizes Example
5.4

and Proposition
5.26
generalizes Example
5.3
.

6.
Coherency preservers on a closed interval

The purpose of the current section is to prove Theorem B.
Before we begin, we give some easy lemmas about intervals.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
, and
P\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
.
Then
[A,B]\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{P}=[A,P]\cup[P,B]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.18
, we may assume
A=0
and
B=I
without loss of generality.
Then
P\in\mathcal{S}_{0,I}=\mathcal{P}
.
It suffices to think of the case
P=E_{11}
.

We need to show that
[0,I]\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}=[0,E_{11}]\cup[E_{11},I]
.
Note that
[0,E_{11}]=\{tE_{11}\,:\,t\in[0,1]\}
and that
[E_{11},I]=\{E_{11}+tE_{22}\,:\,t\in[0,1]\}
.
It is clear that
[0,I]\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}\supset[0,E_{11}]\cup[E_{11},I]
.
Let
X\in[0,I]\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}
.
We show that
X\in[0,E_{11}]\cup[E_{11},I]
.
If
X=E_{11}
, then there is nothing to prove.
So, assume that
X=E_{11}+tQ
for some rank one projection
Q
and some nonzero real number
t
. It suffices to show that
Q\in\{E_{11},E_{22}\}
. If
Q\not=E_{11}
, then
t>0
. Indeed, if
t<0
, then
Q\not=E_{11}
implies that
(Qe_{2},e_{2})>0
and consequently,
(Xe_{2},e_{2})<0
, a contradiction. We have
1\geq(Xe_{1},e_{1})=1+t(Qe_{1},e_{1})
, and consequently
Q=E_{22}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
, and
P\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
.
Then

(6.1)

(A,B]\setminus\ell_{P,B}\subset\bigcup_{X\in[A,P]\setminus\{A,P\}}{\mathcal{C}}_{X}

and

[A,B)\setminus\ell_{A,P}\subset\bigcup_{X\in[P,B]\setminus\{P,B\}}{\mathcal{C}}_{X}

hold.

Proof.

With no loss of generality, we may again assume that
A=0
,
B=I
, and
P=E_{11}
.
To verify the first inclusion, let
X\in(0,I]\setminus\ell_{E_{11},I}
.
We need to find
t\in(0,1)
satisfying
X\sim tE_{11}
.
Since
X>0
, we have
\det X>0
.
On the other hand, we have
((X-E_{11})e_{1},e_{1})=(Xe_{1},e_{1})-1<0
and
((X-E_{11})e_{2},e_{2})=(Xe_{2},e_{2})>0
, thus
X-E_{11}\in H_{2}^{+-}
. It follows that
\det(X-E_{11})<0
.
By the intermediate value theorem, there is
t\in(0,1)
satisfying
\det(X-tE_{11})=0
, or equivalently,
X\sim tE_{11}
.
The proof of the second inclusion is similar.
∎

The above two lemmas clearly imply:

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
, and let
P\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
.
Then every point of
[A,B]
is coherent to some point of
[A,P]
and some point of
[P,B]
.

Remark 6.4
.

When applying the above results, we will often use some straightforward consequences.
For example, it is easy to see that (
6.1
) yields the following.
Every point of
(A,B]
is coherent to some point of
[A,P]\setminus\{A\}
, and every point of
[A,B]\setminus\ell_{P,B}
is coherent to some point of
[A,P]\setminus\{P\}
.

Now, let us begin the proof of Theorem B.
Therefore, suppose that
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
, and that
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is a coherency preserver that is not standard.
We need to show that
\varphi
is either of type
(\mathcal{C})
or
(\ell)
.
We will distinguish three possibilities:
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=0
, or
1
, or
2
.
We will first consider the case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=2
in Subsection
6.1
and then apply the result there to attack the case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))\in\{0,1\}
in Subsections
6.2
,
6.3
.

6.1.
The case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=2

We first deal with the case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=2
.
We would like to show that
\varphi
is degenerate.
In fact, we may give a more precise statement.
To explain it, we make the following definition.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=2
.

•

We say that
\varphi
satisfies
Condition (o)
if the set
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{A,B})
is a singleton
\{Q\}
and
\varphi([A,B])\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}
.

•

We say that
\varphi
satisfies
Condition (i)
if
\varphi([A,B))=\{\varphi(A)\}
and there is no
P\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{A,B}\setminus\{P\})
is a singleton.

•

We say that
\varphi
satisfies
Condition (ii)
if
\varphi((A,B])=\{\varphi(B)\}
and there is no
P\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{A,B}\setminus\{P\})
is a singleton.

•

We say that
\varphi
satisfies
Condition (iii)
if there are
P_{\times}\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
and distinct points
Q_{\circ},Q_{\times}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varphi(A),\varphi(B)}
such that
\varphi([A,B]\cap\ell_{A,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{\varphi(A),Q_{\times}}
and
\varphi([A,B]\setminus\ell_{A,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{Q_{\circ},\varphi(B)}
.

•

We say that
\varphi
satisfies
Condition (iv)
if there are
P_{\times}\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
and distinct points
Q_{\circ},Q_{\times}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varphi(A),\varphi(B)}
such that
\varphi([A,B]\cap\ell_{P_{\times},B})\subset\ell_{Q_{\times},\varphi(B)}
and
\varphi([A,B]\setminus\ell_{P_{\times},B})\subset\ell_{\varphi(A),Q_{\circ}}
.

The main purpose of the current subsection is to show the following proposition.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=2
.
Assume that
\varphi
is not standard.
Then,
\varphi
satisfies (at least) one of Conditions (o)–(iv).

Let
\varphi
be a mapping satisfying the assumption of Proposition
6.6
.
By considering
\psi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
instead of
\varphi
for a pair of suitable automorphisms
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
, we may assume that
A=0
,
B=I
, and
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(I)=I
.
In this case, we have
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}
by Lemma
3.12
.
Therefore, Proposition
6.6
can be obtained from the following.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(I)=I
.
Assume that
\varphi
is not standard. Then (at least) one of the following holds.

•

The mapping
\varphi
satisfies Condition (o), or equivalently, the set
\varphi(\mathcal{P})
is a singleton
\{Q\}
, and
\varphi([0,I])\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}
.

•

The mapping
\varphi
satisfies Condition (i), or equivalently, we have
\varphi([0,I))=\{0\}
, and there is no
P\in\mathcal{P}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\})
is a singleton. In this case, the equality
\varphi(P+cP^{\perp})=\varphi(P)
holds for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
and each
c\in[0,1)
.

•

The mapping
\varphi
satisfies Condition (ii), or equivalently, we have
\varphi((0,I])=\{I\}
, and there is no
P\in\mathcal{P}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\})
is a singleton. In this case, the equality
\varphi(cP)=\varphi(P)
holds for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
and each
c\in(0,1]
.

•

The mapping
\varphi
satisfies Condition (iii), or equivalently, there are
P_{\times},Q_{\circ},Q_{\times}\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
Q_{\circ}\neq Q_{\times}
,
\varphi([0,I]\cap\ell_{0,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{0,Q_{\times}}
, and
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{Q_{\circ},I}
.
In this case, for every
c\in[0,1]
, the set
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{cP_{\times}}\cap[0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}})
is a singleton that consists of the unique point in
\ell_{Q_{\circ},I}
that is coherent to
\varphi(cP_{\times})
.

•

The mapping
\varphi
satisfies Condition (iv), or equivalently, there are
P_{\times},Q_{\circ},Q_{\times}\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
Q_{\circ}\neq Q_{\times}
,
\varphi([0,I]\cap\ell_{P_{\times},I})\subset\ell_{Q_{\times},I}
, and
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{P_{\times},I})\subset\ell_{0,Q_{\circ}}
.
In this case, for every
c\in[0,1]
, the set
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}}\cap[0,I]\setminus\ell_{P_{\times},I})
is a singleton that consists of the unique point in
\ell_{0,Q_{\circ}}
that is coherent to
\varphi(P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp})
.

Remark 6.8
.

When Condition (iii) or (iv) holds, then one may easily see that
\varphi(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{\times}\})=\{Q_{\circ}\}
and
\varphi(P_{\times})=Q_{\times}\neq Q_{\circ}
hold.
Therefore, the point
P_{\times}
(which is an “exceptional” point) is uniquely determined in these cases.

Using Proposition
6.7
, we may see that a mapping satisfying one of Conditions (i)–(iv) is automatically a degenerate coherency preserver.
More precisely, the following holds.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a map with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(I)=I
,
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}
.
If one of the following two conditions holds, then
\varphi
is a degenerate coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

(i)’

\varphi([0,I))=\{0\}
, and
\varphi(P+cP^{\perp})=\varphi(P)
for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
c\in[0,1)
.

(ii)’

\varphi((0,I])=\{I\}
, and
\varphi(cP)=\varphi(P)
for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
c\in(0,1]
.

If one of the following two conditions holds, then
\varphi
is a degenerate coherency preserver of type
(\ell)
.

(iii)’

There are
P_{\times},Q_{\circ},Q_{\times}\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
Q_{\circ}\neq Q_{\times}
,
\varphi([0,I]\cap\ell_{0,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{0,Q_{\times}}
, and
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{Q_{\circ},I}
. Moreover, for every
c\in[0,1]
, the set
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{cP_{\times}}\cap[0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}})
is a singleton that consists of the unique point in
\ell_{Q_{\circ},I}
that is coherent to
\varphi(cP_{\times})
.

(iv)’

There are
P_{\times},Q_{\circ},Q_{\times}\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
Q_{\circ}\neq Q_{\times}
,
\varphi([0,I]\cap\ell_{P_{\times},I})\subset\ell_{Q_{\times},I}
, and
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{P_{\times},I})\subset\ell_{0,Q_{\circ}}
. Moreover, for every
c\in[0,1]
, the set
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}}\cap[0,I]\setminus\ell_{P_{\times},I})
is a singleton that consists of the unique point in
\ell_{0,Q_{\circ}}
that is coherent to
\varphi(P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp})
.

Proof.

If (ii)’ holds, then
\varphi
is the restriction of a mapping described in (1) of Proposition
5.24
.
Thus
\varphi
is a degenerate coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
If (i)’ holds, then one may check that the mapping
X\mapsto I-\varphi(I-X)
is of the form (ii)’, so we again see that
\varphi
is a degenerate coherency preserver of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Assume that (iii)’ holds
and that
A,B\in[0,I]
satisfy
\varphi(A)\not\sim\varphi(B)
.
We see that one of
A,B
lies in
[0,I]\cap\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
and the other is in
[0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
, so (iii)’ clearly implies that
A\not\sim B
.
Thus
\varphi
is a coherency preserver.
It is apparent that
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.
Similarly, we see that
\varphi
is a degenerate coherency preserver of type
(\ell)
if (iv)’ holds.
∎

Proposition
6.7
together with Lemma
6.9
implies the following.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=2
.
Assume that
\varphi
is not standard.
Then
\varphi
is degenerate.

Remark 6.11
.

In Proposition
6.7
, if
j,k\in\{o,i,ii,iii,iv\}
with
j\neq k
and
\{j,k\}\neq\{iii,iv\}
, then it is clear that Conditions (j) and (k) cannot be fulfilled simultaneously.
However, it can happen that (iii) and (iv) are fulfilled simultaneously, in which case
\varphi([0,I])
consists of exactly four points, and
\varphi
is of both types
(\mathcal{C})
and
(\ell)
. To see this, we may assume with no loss of generality that
P_{\times}=Q_{\times}=E_{11}
. (Note that the points
P_{\times}
,
Q_{\circ}
,
Q_{\times}
