in (iii) are the same as those in (iv), see Remark
6.8
.) If
\varphi
satisfies both (iii) and (iv), then all elements of the set
\mathcal{A}=[0,I]\setminus(\ell_{0,E_{11}}\cup\ell_{E_{11},I})
are mapped into the intersection of lines
\ell_{0,Q_{\circ}}
and
\ell_{Q_{\circ},I}
. Thus, we have

(6.2)

\varphi(0)=0,\ \ \ \varphi(I)=I,\ \ \ \varphi(E_{11})=E_{11},\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi(X)=Q_{\circ},\ \ X\in\mathcal{A}.

It remains to consider the
\varphi
-images of elements of the set

\{tE_{11}\,:\,0<t<1\}\cup\{E_{11}+tE_{22}\,:\,0<t<1\}.

For every
t\in(0,1)
, the matrix
tE_{11}
is coherent to
0
,
E_{11}
, and some
X\in\mathcal{A}
. It follows that

(6.3)

\varphi(tE_{11})=0,\ \ 0<t<1.

Similarly, for every
t\in(0,1)
, the matrix
E_{11}+tE_{22}
is coherent to
E_{11}
,
I
, and some
X\in\mathcal{A}
. It follows that

(6.4)

\varphi(E_{11}+tE_{22})=I,\ \ 0<t<1.

Hence, the image of
\varphi
consists of points
0,E_{11},Q_{\circ},I
.
Because
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\{E_{11}\})=\{0,I,Q_{\circ}\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q_{\circ}}
, the map
\varphi
is also of type
(\mathcal{C})
.
Conversely, it is easy to see that
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined by (
6.2
), (
6.3
), and (
6.4
) satisfies (iii) and (iv) simultaneously.

Since the proof of Proposition
6.7
is rather long, we separate it into claims.
In what follows, we assume that
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is a coherency preserving map with
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(I)=I
. We also assume that
\varphi
is not standard.

Claim 6.12
.

We have

(6.5)

\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\subset\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}(\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}\cup\ell_{\varphi(P),I}).

Proof.

Observe that every
A\in[0,I]\setminus(0,I)
is coherent to some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and one of
0,I
, so that we have either
0=\varphi(0)\sim\varphi(A)\sim\varphi(P)
or
I=\varphi(I)\sim\varphi(A)\sim\varphi(P)
.
Since
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}
, we obtain (
6.5
).
∎

Let
A\in(0,I)
. We show

\varphi(A)\in\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}(\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}\cup\ell_{\varphi(P),I})\cup\bigcap_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(P)}.

By Lemma
3.20
, there is an automorphism
\psi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
such that

\psi([0,I])=[0,I],\ \ \psi(0)=0,\ \ \psi(I)=I,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \psi((1/2)I)=A.

By considering
\varphi\circ\psi
instead of
\varphi
, we may assume
A=(1/2)I
without loss of generality.
By Corollary
4.9
, the triple
\varphi(0)=0,\varphi((1/2)I),\varphi(I)=I
is not in timelike position.
Thus we see that one of the following holds:

•

Either
\varphi((1/2)I)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{I}
, or

•

the triple
0,\varphi((1/2)I),I
is in spacelike position.

Claim 6.13
.

If
\varphi((1/2)I)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{I}
, then we have

\varphi((1/2)I)\in\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}\cup\ell_{\varphi(P),I}\right).

Proof.

Assume that
\varphi((1/2)I)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then there are
a\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\setminus\{0\}
and
P_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\varphi((1/2)I)=aP_{0}
.
Fix any
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
We have
0\sim(1/2)Q\sim(1/2)I
, thus

0=\varphi(0)\sim\varphi((1/2)Q)\sim\varphi((1/2)I)=aP_{0}.

It follows that
\varphi((1/2)Q)=bP_{0}
for some
b\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
.
If
b\neq 0
, then
\varphi(Q)=P_{0}
because

bP_{0}=\varphi((1/2)Q)\sim\varphi(Q)\in{\mathcal{P}}.

If
b=0
, then
\varphi(Q^{\perp}+(1/2)Q)=P_{0}
because

aP_{0}=\varphi((1/2)I)\sim\varphi(Q^{\perp}+(1/2)Q)\sim\varphi((1/2)Q)=0

and
\varphi(Q^{\perp}+(1/2)Q)\sim\varphi(I)=I
.
This in turn shows
\varphi(Q^{\perp})=P_{0}
because

P_{0}=\varphi(Q^{\perp}+(1/2)Q)\sim\varphi(Q^{\perp})\in{\mathcal{P}}.

In both cases, we see that
\varphi((1/2)I)=aP_{0}\in\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}
.
Similarly, we obtain
\varphi((1/2)I)\in\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\ell_{\varphi(P),I}
when
\varphi((1/2)I)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{I}\setminus\{I\}
.
∎

As before, we use the symbol
J=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\cr 0&-1\cr\end{matrix}\right]
.

Claim 6.14
.

If the triple
0,\varphi((1/2)I),I
is in spacelike position, then

(6.6)

\varphi((1/2)I)\in\bigcap_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(P)}.

Proof.

Assume that the triple
0,\varphi((1/2)I),I
is in spacelike position.
One may find an automorphism
\psi_{3}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
such that the coherency preserver
\varphi^{\prime}:=\psi_{3}\circ\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies

\varphi^{\prime}(0)=0,\ \ \varphi^{\prime}((1/2)I)=(1/2)J,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi^{\prime}(I)=J

(see Example
3.40
).

Let
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Then we have
\varphi^{\prime}(P)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\varphi^{\prime}(0),\varphi^{\prime}(I)}={\mathcal{S}}_{0,J}
.
Assume that
\varphi^{\prime}(P)\in H_{2}
.
We aim to obtain a contradiction.
Observe that
\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)P)
is the unique point (see Remark
3.26
) that is coherent to the three points

\varphi^{\prime}(0)=0,\ \ \varphi^{\prime}(P),\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi^{\prime}((1/2)I)=(1/2)J.

Since
(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)\sim 0,\varphi^{\prime}(P),(1/2)J
, we obtain
\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)P)=(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)
.
Similarly,
\varphi^{\prime}(P+(1/2)P^{\perp})
is the unique point that is coherent to the three points

\varphi^{\prime}(P),\ \ \varphi^{\prime}(I)=J,\ \ \text{ and }\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)I)=(1/2)J,

which leads to
\varphi^{\prime}(P+(1/2)P^{\perp})=(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)+(1/2)J
.
Then, the fact that
\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)P^{\perp})
is the unique point that is coherent to the three points

\varphi^{\prime}(0)=0,\ \ \varphi^{\prime}((1/2)I)=(1/2)J,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi^{\prime}(P+(1/2)P^{\perp})=(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)+(1/2)J

implies that
\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)P^{\perp})=(1/2)J-(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)
.

By Lemma
3.43
, the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{I}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)P}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)P^{\perp}}\subset[0,I]
is nonempty.
It follows that

\begin{split}\emptyset&\neq\varphi^{\prime}({\mathcal{C}}_{I}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)P}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)P^{\perp}})\\
&\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi^{\prime}(I)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)P)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)P^{\perp})}\\
&={\mathcal{C}}_{J}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)J-(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)}.\end{split}

However, Corollary
3.46
together with Lemma
3.38
shows that

{\mathcal{C}}_{J}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{(1/2)J-(1/2)\varphi^{\prime}(P)}=\emptyset,

so we get to a contradiction.

It follows that
\varphi^{\prime}(P)\notin H_{2}
.
Thus we obtain
\varphi^{\prime}(P)=\infty Q
for some
Q\in\mathcal{P}
because
0=\varphi^{\prime}(0)\sim\varphi^{\prime}(P)
.
Since
J=\varphi^{\prime}(I)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi^{\prime}(P)}={\mathcal{C}}_{\infty Q}
, we have
Q^{\perp}JQ^{\perp}=0
. Thus
Q^{\perp}(1/2)JQ^{\perp}=0
, which in turn implies
(1/2)J\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty Q}={\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi^{\prime}(P)}
.
Since
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
is arbitrary, we arrive at the conclusion
\varphi^{\prime}((1/2)I)=(1/2)J\in\bigcap_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi^{\prime}(P)}
.
This clearly implies (
6.6
).
∎

By the above claims, we get

(6.7)

\varphi([0,I])\subset\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}\cup\ell_{\varphi(P),I}\right)\ \cup\ \bigcap_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(P)}.

Claim 6.15
.

If
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})
is a singleton
\{Q\}
, then
\varphi([0,I])\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}
.

Proof.

By (
6.7
), we get

\varphi([0,I])\subset\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}(\ell_{0,\varphi(P)}\cup\ell_{\varphi(P),I})\cup\bigcap_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(P)}=\ell_{0,Q}\cup\ell_{Q,I}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}={\mathcal{C}}_{Q}.

∎

From now on, we assume that
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})
has at least two points.
Consider the automorphism
\psi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined by
X\mapsto(I-X)^{-1}-I
,
X\in{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
By the definition of the inversion, we have

\psi([0,I])={\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}:=\{aP+bP^{\perp}\,:\,a,b\in[0,\infty],\,P\in{\mathcal{P}}\}.{\@index{\@indexphrase[$Hp$]{${\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}$}}}

In the rest of the proof, to simplify the discussion, we work with the coherency preserver
\Phi:=\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}\colon{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
Note that
\Phi(0)=0
,
\Phi({{\widehat{\infty}}})={{\widehat{\infty}}}
and that
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})\subset\infty{\mathcal{P}}:=\{\infty P\,:\,P\in{\mathcal{P}}\}
.
Therefore, for each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, there is a unique
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\Phi(\infty P)=\infty P^{\prime}
.

For distinct
P,Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
,
we define
{\Pi_{P,Q}^{+}}:={\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\cap\Pi_{P,Q}
, where

\Pi_{P,Q}=\{aP+bQ\,:\,a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}\}\cup\ell_{\infty P,{\widehat{\infty}}}\cup\ell_{\infty Q,{\widehat{\infty}}}

is the surface as in (
3.14
).

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
P,Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
be distinct elements.
Assume that
T
is an invertible
2\times 2
matrix such that
TPT^{*}=E_{11}
and
TQT^{*}=E_{22}
. Let
\psi
denote the automorphism
X\mapsto TXT^{*}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
. Then

•

\psi(\Pi_{P,Q})
is the diagonal surface
\{aE_{11}+bE_{22}\,:\,a,b\in{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}\}
as in (
3.12
), and

•

\psi({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})={\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\psi(\Pi_{P,Q})
is a surface that contains
0,\infty E_{11},\infty E_{22},{\widehat{\infty}}
. By Lemma
3.36
, the diagonal surface is the unique surface that contains these points. Consequently,
\psi(\Pi_{P,Q})
is the diagonal surface.

Let
S
be any invertible
2\times 2
matrix. For
A\in H_{2}
satisfying
A\geq 0
we have
SAS^{\ast}\geq 0
. If
P
is any rank one projection and
b\in[0,\infty]
then
S(\infty P+bP^{\perp})S^{\ast}
is equal to
\infty Q+cQ^{\perp}
for some
Q\in\mathcal{P}
and some
c\in[0,\infty]
. It follows that

\psi({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})\subset{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}
and
\psi^{-1}({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})\subset{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}
, and consequently,
\psi({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})={\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
P,Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
be distinct elements.
Then there is an automorphism of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
that maps
{\Pi_{P,Q}^{+}}
onto
{\square}_{E_{11}}=\{aE_{11}+bE_{22}\,:\,a,b\in[0,1]\}
.

Proof.

We may take an invertible
2\times 2
matrix
T
such that
TPT^{*}=E_{11}
and
TQT^{*}=E_{22}
.
Let
\psi_{1}
denote the affine automorphism
X\mapsto TXT^{*}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
.
By Lemma
6.16
we have
\psi_{1}({\Pi_{P,Q}^{+}})=\{aE_{11}+bE_{22}\,:\,a,b\in[0,\infty]\}
.
Let
\psi_{2}
denote the automorphism
X\ \mapsto I-(I+X)^{-1}
.
Then we obtain
\psi_{2}(\{aE_{11}+bE_{22}\,:\,a,b\in[0,\infty]\})={\square}_{E_{11}}
by definition.
Thus the automorphism
\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}
maps
{\Pi_{P,Q}^{+}}
onto
{\square}_{E_{11}}
.
∎

We study a pair
P_{1},P_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying
P_{1}^{\prime}\neq P_{2}^{\prime}
. Note that the condition
P_{1}^{\prime}\neq P_{2}^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}^{\prime}{P_{2}^{\prime}}^{\perp})\not=0
.
Observe that
0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime},\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}
are contained in
\Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})
.
Since
P_{1}^{\prime}\neq P_{2}^{\prime}
, Lemmas
6.17
,
3.35
, and
3.36
imply that

(6.8)

\Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})\subset\Pi_{P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime}}.

Claim 6.18
.

We have

(6.9)

\Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})\subset\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}\cup\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}.

Proof.

Observe that (
6.7
) assures that

\Phi({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})\subset\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}(\ell_{0,\infty P^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}})\cup\bigcap_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty P^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{\widehat{\infty}}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}.

On the other hand, it is clear that

{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cap\Pi_{P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime}}=\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}},\ \ {\mathcal{C}}_{{\widehat{\infty}}}\cap\Pi_{P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime}}=\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}},

and

\mathcal{C}_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cap\Pi_{P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime}}=\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}

hold.
Therefore, (
6.8
) leads to (
6.9
).
∎

For each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we have
\Phi(\ell_{0,\infty P}\cap{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})\subset\ell_{0,\infty P^{\prime}}
, so we may define a function
f_{P}\colon[0,\infty]\to{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
by

\Phi(tP)=f_{P}(t)P^{\prime},\ \ t\in[0,\infty].

Similarly, we may define a function
g_{P}\colon[0,\infty]\to{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
by

\Phi(\infty P+tP^{\perp})=\infty P^{\prime}+g_{P}(t){P^{\prime}}^{\perp},\ \ t\in[0,\infty].

Note that
f_{P}(0)=g_{P}(0)=0
and
f_{P}(\infty)=g_{P}(\infty)=\infty
hold.

In what follows, we apply the rule
c\cdot\infty=\infty\cdot c=\infty
when
c\in(0,\infty)
.
Let
t\in[0,\infty]
.
Since
tP_{1}\sim\infty P_{2}+t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp}
, we obtain

f_{P_{1}}(t)P_{1}^{\prime}=\Phi(tP_{1})\sim\Phi(\infty P_{2}+t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp})=\infty P_{2}^{\prime}+g_{P_{2}}(t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})){P_{2}^{\prime}}^{\perp},

which in turn implies

(6.10)

f_{P_{1}}(t)\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}^{\prime}{P_{2}^{\prime}}^{\perp})=g_{P_{2}}(t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})).

Note that equality (
6.10
) is valid in the case
f_{P_{1}}(t)=\infty
or
g_{P_{2}}(t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp}))=\infty
, too.
Actually, in the case when
f_{P_{1}}(t)\in\{0,\infty\}
or
g_{P_{2}}(t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp}))\in\{0,\infty\}
we have

(6.11)

f_{P_{1}}(t)=g_{P_{2}}(t\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})).

Claim 6.19
.

Either

(6.12)

f_{P_{1}}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})\subset\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}},

or

(6.13)

g_{P_{1}}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})\subset\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}

holds.

Proof.

Assume that there is a real number
a\in(0,\infty)
such that
f_{P_{1}}(a)\notin\{0,\infty\}
.
Put
A_{1}:=aP_{1}
and
A_{2}:=\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp}
.
Set
B_{1}:=\Phi(A_{1})=f_{P_{1}}(a)P_{1}^{\prime}
and
B_{2}:=\Phi(A_{2})=\infty P_{2}^{\prime}+g_{P_{2}}(a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})){P_{2}^{\prime}}^{\perp}
.
Then (
6.10
) implies
B_{2}=\infty P_{2}^{\prime}+f_{P_{1}}(a)\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}^{\prime}{P_{2}^{\prime}}^{\perp}){P_{2}^{\prime}}^{\perp}
.
It follows that

\ell_{B_{1},B_{2}}=\{f_{P_{1}}(a)P_{1}^{\prime}+bP_{2}^{\prime}\,:\,b\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{B_{2}\}.

Since
f_{P_{1}}(a)\notin\{0,\infty\}
, (
6.9
) implies

(6.14)

\Phi(\ell_{A_{1},A_{2}}\cap{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})\subset\ell_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap(\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}\cup\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}})=\{B_{1},B_{2}\}.

Since
A_{1},A_{2}\in{\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}}
and
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
,
Lemma
6.17
combined with Lemma
3.34
implies that each element in
{\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}}
is coherent to some element of
\ell_{A_{1},A_{2}}\cap{\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}}
.
This together with (
6.9
) and (
6.14
) yields

\Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})\subset({\mathcal{C}}_{B_{1}}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{B_{2}})\cap(\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}\cup\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}})=\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}.

In particular, for every
t\in[0,\infty]
we have

\Phi(\infty P_{1}+tP_{1}^{\perp})=\infty P_{1}^{\prime}+g_{P_{1}}(t){P_{1}^{\prime}}^{\perp}\in\ell_{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}.

Consequently,
g_{P_{1}}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
holds.
Thus (
6.13
) is established in this case.

Assume that
f_{P_{1}}((0,\infty))\subset\{0,\infty\}
.
Let
a\in[0,\infty]
.
By
aP_{1}\sim\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp}
, we have

\Phi(\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp})\sim\Phi(aP_{1})\in\{0,\infty P_{1}^{\prime}\}.

This together with

\Phi(\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp})\in\ell_{\Phi(\infty P_{2}),\Phi({\widehat{\infty}})}=\ell_{\infty P_{2}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}

implies that
\Phi(\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp})=\infty P_{2}^{\prime}
when
\Phi(aP_{1})=0
and
\Phi(\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp})={\widehat{\infty}}
when
\Phi(aP_{1})=\infty P_{1}^{\prime}
.
It follows that

\Phi(\ell_{aP_{1},\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp}}\cap{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}})\subset\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}.

Every point of
{\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}}
lies in
\ell_{aP_{1},\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp}}
for some
a\in[0,\infty]
. (To see this, one can use the automorphism
\psi_{1}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined as in the proof of Lemma 6.12 with
P_{1},P_{2}
instead of
P,Q
, which maps
{\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}}
onto
\{aE_{11}+bE_{22}\,:\,a,b\in[0,\infty]\}
and the line

\ell_{aP_{1},\infty P_{2}+a\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}P_{2}^{\perp})P_{2}^{\perp}}
onto the line
\ell_{aE_{11},\infty E_{22}+aE_{11}}
.)
Consequently,
\Phi({\Pi_{P_{1},P_{2}}^{+}})\subset\ell_{0,\infty P_{2}^{\prime}}\cup\ell_{\infty P_{1}^{\prime},{\widehat{\infty}}}
in this case.
Thus (
6.12
) is established.
∎

Claim 6.20
.

If the set
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})
(or equivalently,
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})
) has at least three elements, then
f_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
and
g_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
hold for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proof.

For
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we have
f_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
or
g_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
by Claim
6.19
.
In the latter case, take
P_{1},P_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
P^{\prime}\neq P_{1}^{\prime}\neq P_{2}^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime}
.
Then equation (
6.11
) implies
f_{P_{1}}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
, and similarly, we obtain
g_{P_{2}}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
, and then
f_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
.
Therefore, we see that
f_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
holds for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Similarly, we have
g_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
.
∎

The same argument shows the following.

Claim 6.21
.

Assume that the set
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})
(or equivalently,
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})
) has at least three elements.

•

If
f_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
or
g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, then
f_{P}((0,\infty))=g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
holds for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.

•

If
f_{P}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
or
g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, then
f_{P}((0,\infty))=g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
holds for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.

Claim 6.22
.

Assume that
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
satisfies
f_{P}([0,\infty])=\{0,\infty\}
.
Assume in addition that for any
c\in(0,1)
there exists
Q_{c}\in{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying
\mathrm{tr}\,(PQ_{c})=c
and
P^{\prime}\neq Q_{c}^{\prime}\neq(P^{\perp})^{\prime}
.
Then
f_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
or
f_{P}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
holds.

Proof.

We have
\mathrm{tr}\,(PQ_{c}^{\perp})=1-c
and
\mathrm{tr}\,(P^{\perp}Q_{c}^{\perp})=c
.
Therefore, (
6.11
) implies that

\{0,\infty\}\ni f_{P}(t)=g_{Q_{c}}((1-c)t)=f_{P^{\perp}}\left(\frac{1-c}{c}t\right)

for every
t\in[0,\infty]
and
c\in(0,1)
.
Let
t\in(0,\infty)
. Then there is
c\in(0,1)
such that
((1-c)/c)t=1
.
Thus
f_{P}(t)=f_{P^{\perp}}(1)\in\{0,\infty\}
for every
t\in(0,\infty)
.
∎

In the same way, we get the following.

Claim 6.23
.

Assume that
R\in{\mathcal{P}}
satisfies
g_{R}([0,\infty])=\{0,\infty\}
.
Assume in addition that for any
c\in(0,1)
there exists
Q_{c}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\mathrm{tr}\,(RQ_{c})=c
and
R^{\prime}\neq Q_{c}^{\prime}\neq(R^{\perp})^{\prime}
.
Then
g_{R}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
or
g_{R}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
holds.

Claim 6.24
.

Assume that there is no
P\in\mathcal{P}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\})
is a singleton.

•

If
f_{P}(t)=0
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and every
t\in(0,\infty)
, then (i) holds.

•

If
f_{P}(t)=\infty
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and every
t\in(0,\infty)
, then (ii) holds.

Proof.

Assume
f_{P}(t)=0
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and every
t\in(0,\infty)
.
Since
\Phi({\mathcal{P}})
is not a singleton, an application of (
6.11
) implies that
g_{P}(t)=0
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and every
t\in(0,\infty)
.
These equations mean that
\varphi(cP)=0
and
\varphi(P+cP^{\perp})=\varphi(P)\in{\mathcal{P}}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and every
c\in(0,1)
.
Let
X\in(0,I)
.
For each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we may find
c,d\in(0,1)
such that
cP\sim X\sim P+dP^{\perp}
(Lemma
6.2
).
It follows that

0=\varphi(cP)\sim\varphi(X)\sim\varphi(P+dP^{\perp})=\varphi(P).

This implies
\varphi(X)=0
because
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}
has at least two elements.
Thus we have shown that (i) holds in this case.
Similarly, we see that (ii) holds if
f_{P}(t)=\infty
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and every
t\in(0,\infty)
.
∎

Let us continue with a very simple lemma.

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
m\colon(0,\infty)\to\{0,\infty\}
and
g\colon(0,\infty)\to{{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}}
be two functions and
a,b
real numbers with
0<a<b
. Assume that for every
t\in(0,\infty)
and every
s\in(a,b)
we have

m(t)=g(ts).

Then either
g(t)=m(t)=0
for every
t\in(0,\infty)
, or
g(t)=m(t)=\infty
for every
t\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that both sets
\{t\in(0,\infty)\,:\,m(t)=0\}
and
\{t\in(0,\infty)\,:\,m(t)=\infty\}
are open. Since
(0,\infty)
is connected, one of these two sets is equal to
(0,\infty)
.
∎

Claim 6.26
.

If the set
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})
has at least three elements, then (i) or (ii) holds.

Proof.

By Claim
6.20
,
f_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
and
g_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
hold for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Assume that
\Phi
restricted to
\infty{\mathcal{P}}
is injective.
Then Claims
6.22
,
6.21
, and
6.24
imply that (i) or (ii) holds.

In the rest of this proof, we assume that there is a pair of distinct elements
P_{1},P_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
P_{1}^{\prime}=P_{2}^{\prime}
.
By considering
\Phi\circ\psi_{1}
for an appropriate affine automorphism
\psi_{1}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
of the form
X\mapsto SXS^{*}
for some invertible
2\times 2
complex matrix
S
,
we may assume that
P_{2}=P_{1}^{\perp}
without loss of generality.
Assume that for each
c\in(0,1)
there is
Q_{c}\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q_{c})=c
and
Q_{c}^{\prime}\neq P_{1}^{\prime}=(P_{1}^{\perp})^{\prime}
.
Applying Claims
6.22
,

6.21
, and
6.24
again,
we see that either (i) or (ii) holds.

From now on, we assume that there is
c\in(0,1)
such that every projection
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q)=c
satisfies
Q^{\prime}=P_{1}^{\prime}
.
Assume that there is a pair of distinct points
P_{3},P_{4}\in{\mathcal{P}}
such that
P_{3}^{\prime}\neq P_{1}^{\prime}\neq P_{4}^{\prime}
and the set

(6.15)

\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{4}^{\perp})}{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{3}^{\perp})}\,:\,Q\in{\mathcal{P}},\,\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q)=c\right\}

has nonempty interior.
Then,
P_{3}^{\prime}\neq Q^{\prime}\neq P_{4}^{\prime}
, so (
6.11
) implies that

(6.16)

\{0,\infty\}\ni g_{P_{3}}(t)=f_{Q}\left(\frac{t}{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{3}^{\perp})}\right)=g_{P_{4}}\left(t\frac{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{4}^{\perp})}{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{3}^{\perp})}\right)

for every
t\in[0,\infty]
and
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q)=c
.
Using Lemma
6.25
, we see that
g_{P_{3}}
is constantly
0
or constantly
\infty
on
(0,\infty)
.
It follows from Claims
6.21
and
6.24
that we may obtain the same conclusion as in the preceding paragraphs.

If there is no pair
P_{3},P_{4}
with the above property, then the technical lemma below (Lemma
6.27
) implies that
P^{\prime}=P_{1}^{\prime}
holds for all but exactly two points in
{\mathcal{P}}
. (Here we used the assumption that
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})
has at least three points.)
In this case, let
P_{5}
and
P_{6}
be the exceptional points.
Considering
\Phi\circ\psi_{2}
for an appropriate affine automorphism
\psi_{2}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}

instead of
\Phi
, we may assume that
P_{6}=P_{5}^{\perp}
without loss of generality. We have
P_{5}^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime}\neq(P_{5}^{\perp})^{\prime}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{5},P_{5}^{\perp}\}
.
Let
d
be any real number,
0<d<1
. For every
Q\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{5}Q)=d
, we have
P_{5}\neq Q\neq P_{5}^{\perp}
and therefore
P_{5}^{\prime}\neq Q^{\prime}\neq(P_{5}^{\perp})^{\prime}
.
Thus we may once again apply Claims
6.22
,
6.21
, and
6.24
to obtain the same conclusion.
∎

Lemma 6.27
.

Let
P_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
0<c<1
.
Set
\mathcal{R}:=\{Q\in{\mathcal{P}}\,:\,\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q)=c\}
.
For any
P_{3}\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus(\{P_{1},P_{1}^{\perp}\}\cup\mathcal{R})
, there is at most one point
P_{4}\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus(\{P_{1},P_{1}^{\perp},P_{3}\}\cup\mathcal{R})
with the property that the set

(6.17)

\left\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{4}^{\perp})}{\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{3}^{\perp})}\,:\,Q\in\mathcal{R}\right\}

has empty interior.

Proof.

We may assume
P_{1}=E_{11}
without loss of generality.
In this case, we have

\mathcal{R}=\left\{\left[\begin{matrix}c&e^{it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}\cr e^{-it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}&1-c\cr\end{matrix}\right]\,:\,t\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

For a pair of elements
P_{3},P_{4}\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus(\{P_{1},P_{1}^{\perp}\}\cup\mathcal{R})
,
there are
c_{j}\in(0,1)
with
c_{j}\neq c
and
t_{j}\in[0,2\pi)
such that

P_{j}=\left[\begin{matrix}c_{j}&e^{it_{j}}\sqrt{c_{j}-c_{j}^{2}}\cr e^{-it_{j}}\sqrt{c_{j}-c_{j}^{2}}&1-c_{j}\cr\end{matrix}\right],\ \ j=3,4.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
and

Q=\left[\begin{matrix}c&e^{it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}\cr e^{-it}\sqrt{c-c^{2}}&1-c\cr\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathcal{R}.

Then

\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{j}^{\perp})=c+c_{j}-2cc_{j}-2\cos(t-t_{j})\sqrt{(c-c^{2})(c_{j}-c_{j}^{2})}.

We have
c+c_{j}-2cc_{j}>0
.
Note that
P_{j}\notin\mathcal{R}
implies
\mathrm{tr}\,(QP_{j}^{\perp})\not=0
,
j=3,4
, for all
Q\in\mathcal{R}
. We see that the set (
6.17
) is equal to the range of the continuous function
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

f(t)={a_{4}-b_{4}\cos(t-t_{4})\over a_{3}-b_{3}\cos(t-t_{3})}={g_{4}(t)\over g_{3}(t)},

where

a_{j}=c+c_{j}-2cc_{j},\ \ \ b_{j}=2\sqrt{(c-c^{2})(c_{j}-c_{j}^{2})},\ \ \text{and}\ \ g_{j}(t)=a_{j}-b_{j}\cos(t-t_{j})

for
j=3,4
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Therefore, the set (
6.17
) has empty interior if and only if
f
is a constant function, that is,
g_{3}
and
g_{4}
are linearly dependent.
Assume that these equivalent conditions hold. Then, using the fact that
a_{3},a_{4},b_{3},b_{4}
are positive together with the assumption
0\leq t_{3},t_{4}<2\pi
, and considering the behavior of the functions
g_{3},g_{4}
, it is easily seen that
t_{3}=t_{4}
and
a_{4}/a_{3}=b_{4}/b_{3}>0
.
It follows that

\frac{c+c_{4}-2cc_{4}}{c+c_{3}-2cc_{3}}=\frac{\sqrt{c_{4}-c_{4}^{2}}}{\sqrt{c_{3}-c_{3}^{2}}},

hence

\left(\frac{c+c_{4}-2cc_{4}}{c+c_{3}-2cc_{3}}\right)^{2}=\frac{c_{4}-c_{4}^{2}}{c_{3}-c_{3}^{2}}.

Observe that for a fixed
c_{3}
this may be viewed as a quadratic equation with respect to
c_{4}
one of whose solution is
c_{3}
.
Thus the desired conclusion is obtained.
∎

In what follows, we assume that the set
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})
(or equivalently,
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})
) has exactly two elements.
Let
\Phi(\infty{\mathcal{P}})
consist of exactly two points
\infty Q_{1},\infty Q_{2}
with
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Set
\mathcal{Q}:=\{P\in{\mathcal{P}}\,:\,P^{\prime}=Q_{1}\}
.

Claim 6.28
.

Assume that both
\mathcal{Q}
and
{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
have at least two points.
Then (i) or (ii) holds.

Proof.

Apply Claim
6.19
together with (
6.11
) to see the following.
By exchanging the roles of
\mathcal{Q}
and
{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
if necessary, we may assume
f_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
for every
P\in\mathcal{Q}

and
g_{P}([0,\infty])\subset\{0,\infty\}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
.

From here, we go along more or less the same lines as in the proof of the preceding claim for a while.
Take distinct elements
P_{1},P_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Considering
\Phi\circ\psi_{1}
for an appropriate affine automorphism
\psi_{1}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
instead of
\Phi
, we may assume that
P_{2}=P_{1}^{\perp}
without loss of generality.

Assume that for each
c\in(0,1)
there is
Q_{c}\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q_{c})=c
.
Using Claim
6.22
, we see that
f_{P_{1}}(t)
is constantly
0
on
(0,\infty)
or constantly
\infty
on
(0,\infty)
.

In the current and the next paragraph, we assume that there is
c\in(0,1)
such that every projection
Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q)=c
is in
\mathcal{Q}
.
Assume additionally that there is a pair of distinct points
P_{3},P_{4}\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
such that the set (
6.15
) has nonempty interior.
Then, (
6.11
) implies (
6.16
)
for every
t\in[0,\infty]
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{1}Q)=c
.
Using Lemma
6.25
, we see that
g_{P_{3}}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
or
g_{P_{3}}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
holds.

Assume that there is no pair
P_{3},P_{4}
with the above property.
Lemma
6.27
implies that
P\in\mathcal{Q}
holds for all but exactly two points in
{\mathcal{P}}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{Q}=\{R,R_{1}\}
with
R\not=R_{1}
.
As in the second paragraph of the proof, there is no loss of generality in assuming that
R_{1}=R^{\perp}
. Clearly, for every
c\in(0,1)
there exists
Q_{c}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
\mathrm{tr}\,(RQ_{c})=c
. Moreover,
R^{\prime}\not=Q_{c}^{\prime}\not=(R^{\perp})^{\prime}
.
Using Claim
6.23
, we see that
g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
or
g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{\infty\}
holds for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
.

Therefore, in all cases, by using (
6.11
), we see that one of the following holds.

•

f_{Q}(t)=g_{P}(t)=0
for every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
,
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
, and
t\in(0,\infty)
, or

•

f_{Q}(t)=g_{P}(t)=\infty
for every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
,
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
, and
t\in(0,\infty)
.

Let us assume that the first condition holds.
Let
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Then we may find
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
that is different from
P
.
For each
t\in(0,\infty)
, we have
\infty P+t\mathrm{tr}\,(Q{P}^{\perp})P^{\perp}\sim tQ
and
\Phi(tQ)=0
, thus we get
\Phi(\infty P+t\mathrm{tr}\,(Q{P}^{\perp})P^{\perp})\sim 0
.
It follows that
g_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, hence (
6.11
) implies
f_{P}((0,\infty))=\{0\}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Thus Claim
6.24
implies that (i) holds.
Similarly, we obtain (ii) in the latter case.
∎

We consider the remaining case.
Therefore, we assume that either
\mathcal{Q}
or
{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
has only one point.
By exchanging the roles of
\mathcal{Q}
and
{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
if necessary, we may assume that
\mathcal{Q}
has only one point
P_{\times}
.
Put
Q_{\times}:=(P_{\times})^{\prime}
and
Q_{\circ}:=P^{\prime}
for any
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
.

Claim 6.29
.

Either
f_{P_{\times}}([0,\infty))=\{0\}
, or
g_{P_{\times}}((0,\infty])=\{\infty\}
holds.

Proof.

Fix a point
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
and a real number
t\in(0,\infty)
.
Let
R\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
.
We have

\Phi(tP)\sim\infty Q_{\circ}+g_{R}(t\mathrm{tr}\,(PR^{\perp}))Q_{\circ}^{\perp}

because
tP\sim\infty R+t\mathrm{tr}\,(PR^{\perp})R^{\perp}
.
Moreover, by (
6.10
), we obtain

(6.18)

f_{P_{\times}}(s)\mathrm{tr}\,(Q_{\times}Q_{\circ}^{\perp})=g_{R}(s\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{\times}R^{\perp}))

for every
s\in[0,\infty]
.
It follows that

\Phi(tP)\sim\infty Q_{\circ}+f_{P_{\times}}\left(t\frac{\mathrm{tr}\,(PR^{\perp})}{\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{\times}R^{\perp})}\right)\mathrm{tr}\,(Q_{\times}Q_{\circ}^{\perp})Q_{\circ}^{\perp}.

Note that the number
{\mathrm{tr}\,(PR^{\perp})}/{\mathrm{tr}\,(P_{\times}R^{\perp})}
can take all values of
[0,\infty)
as
R
runs over
{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
.

Therefore, if
f_{P_{\times}}
restricted to
[0,\infty)
is not a constant function, then
\Phi(tP)
needs to lie in
\ell_{\infty Q_{\circ},{\widehat{\infty}}}
, which together with
0\sim tP
implies
\Phi(tP)=\infty Q_{\circ}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
and
t\in(0,\infty)
. In this case, (
6.11
) implies
g_{P_{\times}}(t)=\infty
for every
t\in(0,\infty)
.
∎

The following claim completes the proof of Proposition
6.7
.

Claim 6.30
.

If
f_{P_{\times}}([0,\infty))=\{0\}
, then (iv) holds.
If
g_{P_{\times}}((0,\infty])=\{\infty\}
, then (iii) holds.

Proof.

We only confirm the latter statement because the other can be verified in a parallel manner.
Thus we assume
g_{P_{\times}}((0,\infty])=\{\infty\}
.
In this case, we have
\Phi(\infty P_{\times}+tP_{\times}^{\perp})={\widehat{\infty}}
for every
t\in(0,\infty]
.
It is clear from the definition of the coherency relation that every element of
{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\setminus\ell_{0,\infty P_{\times}}
is coherent to
\infty P_{\times}+tP_{\times}^{\perp}
for some
t\in(0,\infty]
.
Thus, we get
\Phi({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\setminus\ell_{0,\infty P_{\times}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{{\widehat{\infty}}}
.

By (
6.11
), we have
\Phi(tP)=\infty Q_{\circ}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
and
t\in(0,\infty]
.
On the other hand, we see that every
A\in{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\setminus\ell_{0,\infty P_{\times}}
is coherent to
tP
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
and
t\in(0,\infty]
.
Indeed, this is clear if
A\sim 0
.
If
A\not\sim 0
, then
A
is coherent to
tP_{\times}^{\perp}
for some
t\in(0,\infty]
.
Therefore, we get
\Phi({\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\setminus\ell_{0,\infty P_{\times}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty Q_{\circ}}
.

For any
c\in[0,\infty]
, we have
\Phi(cP_{\times})\in\ell_{0,\infty Q_{\times}}
, so
\Phi(cP_{\times})\not\in\ell_{\infty Q_{\circ},{{\widehat{\infty}}}}
. Hence, any
A\in{\overline{H_{2}^{+}}}\setminus\ell_{0,\infty P_{\times}}
that is coherent to
cP_{\times}
is mapped by
\Phi
to the unique point on the line
\ell_{\infty Q_{\circ},{{\widehat{\infty}}}}
that is coherent to
\Phi(cP_{\times})
.
It is now straightforward to deduce that (iii) holds.
∎

Remark 6.31
.

In the language of
\varphi
, the condition
g_{P_{\times}}((0,\infty])=\{\infty\}
in Claim
6.30
means that
\varphi(P_{\times}+tP_{\times}^{\perp})=\varphi(I)=I
for every
t\in(0,1)
.

Before we close this subsection, we give two lemmas which will be used in the subsequent arguments.

Lemma 6.32
.

Let
j\in\{o,i,ii,iii,iv\}
.
Let
A,B,C\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<C\leq B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=d(\varphi(A),\varphi(C))=2
.
Assume that
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies Condition (j).
Then
\varphi
restricted to
[A,C]
also satisfies Condition (j).

Proof.

By Corollary
3.54
, we see that
\varphi
restricted to
[A,C]
is degenerate. Thus, this restriction satisfies (at least) one of the five Conditions (o), (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv). For each
P\in\mathcal{S}_{A,C}
, there is a unique
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
such that
P\sim P^{\prime}
. Clearly,
P^{\prime}
is the unique point on
\ell_{A,P}
that is coherent to
B
. The correspondence
P\mapsto P^{\prime}
is a bijection of

\mathcal{S}_{A,C}
onto
\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
.
Indeed, for
R\in\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
, we take the unique point
Q
on the line
\ell_{A,R}
that is coherent to
C
. Then
Q^{\prime}=R
.

The point
\varphi(P^{\prime})
is the unique point on
\ell_{\varphi(A),\varphi(P)}
that is coherent to
\varphi(B)
. It follows that for
P,Q\in\mathcal{S}_{A,C}
we have
\varphi(P)=\varphi(Q)
if and only if

\varphi(P^{\prime})=\varphi(Q^{\prime})
. Hence, if
\varphi
satisfies (o), then the restriction of
\varphi
to
[A,C]
satisfies (o), and if
\varphi
satisfies (i) or (ii), then the restriction of
\varphi
to
[A,C]
satisfies (i) or (ii), and
if
\varphi
satisfies (iii) or (iv), then the restriction of
\varphi
to
[A,C]
satisfies (iii) or (iv). It is trivial to see that if
\varphi
satisfies (j),
j\in\{i,ii\}
, then
\varphi
restricted to
[A,C]
satisfies (j).

Assume next that
\varphi
satisfies (iii).
Then we have
\varphi([A,B]\cap\ell_{A,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{\varphi(A),Q_{\times}}
and
\varphi([A,B]\setminus\ell_{A,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{Q_{\circ},\varphi(B)}
.
It follows that
\varphi([A,C]\cap\ell_{A,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{\varphi(A),Q_{\times}}
and
\varphi([A,C]\setminus\ell_{A,P_{\times}})\subset\ell_{Q_{\circ},\varphi(B)}
.
From this, it is straightforward to see that
\varphi
restricted to
[A,C]
also satisfies (iii).

Finally, assume that
\varphi
satisfies (iv).
Then we have
\varphi([A,B]\cap\ell_{P_{\times},B})\subset\ell_{Q_{\times},\varphi(B)}
and
\varphi([A,B]\setminus\ell_{P_{\times},B})\subset\ell_{\varphi(A),Q_{\circ}}
.
In this case, the assumption
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(C))=2
implies that
C\in\ell_{P_{\times},B}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
.
Thus we have
\ell_{P_{\times},C}=\ell_{P_{\times},B}
and
\ell_{Q_{\times},\varphi(C)}=\ell_{Q_{\times},\varphi(B)}
, and it is not hard to see that
\varphi
restricted to
[A,C]
satisfies (iv).
∎

In the same way, we get the following lemma.

Lemma 6.33
.

Let
j\in\{o,i,ii,iii,iv\}
.
Let
A,B,C\in H_{2}
satisfy
A\leq C<B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=d(\varphi(C),\varphi(B))=2
.
Assume that
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfies Condition (j).
Then
\varphi
restricted to
[C,B]
also satisfies Condition (j).

6.2.
The case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=0

We consider the case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=0
, or equivalently,
\varphi(A)=\varphi(B)
.
In this case, we have either
\varphi([A,B])\subset\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(A)}=\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(B)}
, or there is
C\in(A,B)
such that
d(\varphi(C),\varphi(A))=2
.
In the former case,
\varphi
is clearly of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

We study the latter case.
By considering
\psi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
for a pair of suitable automorphisms
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
, we may assume that
A=-I
,
B=I
, and
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(-I)=\varphi(I)=I
(use Corollary
3.21
).

Proposition 6.34
.

Let
\varphi\colon[-I,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(-I)=\varphi(I)=I
.
Then
\varphi
is degenerate.

To prove this proposition, let us consider a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon[-I,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(-I)=\varphi(I)=I
.
Observe that
\varphi
is not standard because
\varphi(-I)=\varphi(I)
.
By Corollary
3.54
we see that
\varphi
is not standard on every nonempty open subset of
[-I,I]
.
We have
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}
and
\varphi(-{\mathcal{P}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}
by the assumption.
For each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we have
P\sim-P
and thus
\varphi(P)\sim\varphi(-P)
.
Since
\varphi(P),\varphi(-P)\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we obtain
\varphi(P)=\varphi(-P)
.

The restriction
\varphi_{1}
of
\varphi
to
[0,I]
, and the mapping
\varphi_{2}\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
defined by
\varphi_{2}(X)=\varphi(-X)
,
X\in[0,I]
, satisfy the assumption of Proposition
6.7
.
Moreover, we know that
\varphi(P)=\varphi_{1}(P)=\varphi_{2}(P)
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
By considering the mapping
X\mapsto\varphi(-X)
instead of
\varphi
, if necessary, we see that we only need to consider the following seven possibilities:

(I)

Both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (o).

(II)

Both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (i).

(III)

The maps
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (i) and Condition (ii), respectively.

(IV)

Both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (ii).

(V)

Both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (iii).

(VI)

The maps
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (iii) and Condition (iv), respectively.

(VII)

Both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (iv).

Let us study each case.

Claim 6.35
.

If (I) holds, then
\varphi([-I,I])
is contained in one cone and
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Assume that both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy Condition (o).
Then we have
\varphi([-I,0]\cup[0,I])\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}
and
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})=\{Q\}
.
For every
X\in[-I,I]\setminus([-I,0]\cup[0,I])
we have
X\not\in(0,I)
,
X\leq I
, and
X\not<0
, and so Lemma
3.44
implies that
X
is coherent to some point of
{\mathcal{P}}
.
Thus
\varphi([-I,I])\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}
.
∎

Claim 6.36
.

Condition (II) never holds.

Proof.

If both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy (i), then we have
\varphi([0,I)\cup(-I,0])=\{0\}
.
For each real number
c\in(0,2)
and
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we see that
-I+cP^{\perp}
is coherent to some point of
[0,I)\cup(-I,0]
.
By
-P\sim-I+cP^{\perp}\sim-I
and

\varphi([0,I)\cup(-I,0])=\{0\},\ \ \varphi(-P)=\varphi(P)\in{\mathcal{P}},\ \ \varphi(-I)=I,

we have
\varphi(-I+cP^{\perp})=\varphi(P)\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
Similarly, we have
\varphi(I-cP^{\perp})=\varphi(P)\in{\mathcal{P}}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
c\in(0,2)
.
By Lemma
6.2
, for every pair
P,Q\in{\mathcal{P}}
,
P\not=Q^{\perp}
and
c\in(0,2)
, there is
d\in(0,2)
such that
-I+cP^{\perp}\sim I-dQ^{\perp}
.
Thus
\varphi(P)\in{\mathcal{P}}
is coherent to
\varphi(Q)\in{\mathcal{P}}
, hence
\varphi(P)=\varphi(Q)
whenever
P\not=Q^{\perp}
.
This yields that
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})
is a singleton, which contradicts our assumption.
∎

Claim 6.37
.

Condition (III) never holds.

Proof.

If
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (i) and
\varphi_{2}
satisfies (ii), then we see that
\varphi([0,I))=\{0\}
and
\varphi([-I,0))=\{I\}
. Set
\mathcal{N}:=(-I,I)\cap H_{2}^{+-}
. Each
A\in\mathcal{N}
is coherent to some scalar matrix in
(0,I)
and some scalar matrix in
(-I,0)
and therefore
\varphi(\mathcal{N})\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{0,I}={\mathcal{P}}
.
Recall that any two distinct elements in
{\mathcal{P}}
are not coherent.
On the other hand,
\mathcal{N}
is open and connected, so Lemma
4.10
implies that
\varphi(\mathcal{N})
is a singleton
\{Q\}
in
\mathcal{P}
. For every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we have

\varphi(P)=\varphi(P+(1/2)P^{\perp})
because
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (i), while

\varphi(P+(1/2)P^{\perp})\sim\varphi(-(1/2)P+(1/2)P^{\perp})\in\varphi(\mathcal{N})=\{Q\}.

This further implies that
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})=\{Q\}
and we again get a contradiction.
∎

Claim 6.38
.

If (IV) holds, then
\varphi([-I,I]\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
, thus
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Assume that both
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
satisfy (ii).
Then we have
\varphi([-I,0)\cup(0,I])=\{I\}
.
Since every point of
[-I,I]\setminus\{0\}
is coherent to some point in
[-I,0)\cup(0,I]
, we have
\varphi([-I,I]\setminus\{0\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{I}
.
∎

In what follows, we assume that
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (iii) or (iv).
Then there is
P_{\times}\in\mathcal{P}
such that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{\times}\})
is a singleton
\{Q_{\circ}\}
and
Q_{\times}:=\varphi(P_{\times})\neq Q_{\circ}
.
Since
\varphi(P)=\varphi(-P)
for every
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we obtain
\varphi(-\mathcal{P}\setminus\{-P_{\times}\})=\{Q_{\circ}\}
and
\varphi(-P_{\times})=Q_{\times}
.

Lemma 6.39
.

If
X\in[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
, then
X
is coherent to some point of
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cap[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
.

Proof.

We may write
X=aP+bP^{\perp}
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
and
a,b\in[-1,1]
,
a\neq 0
. Thus
X
is coherent to
aP\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cap[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.40
.

If
X\in[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
, then
X
is coherent to some point of

\{P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}\,:\,c\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}\}\cup\{-P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}\,:\,c\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}\}.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.3
, we know that every point of
[-I,I]\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{P_{\times}}
is coherent to some point of
\{P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}\,:\,c\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}\}
, and every point of
[-I,I]\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{-P_{\times}}
is coherent to some point of
\{-P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}\,:\,c\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}\}
.
Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{P_{\times}}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{-P_{\times}}=\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
, we get to the desired conclusion.
∎

Claim 6.41
.

If (V), (VI), or (VII) holds, then
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.

Proof.

Let
b\in(0,1]
.
Then
-P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp}
is coherent to
-I
and
-P_{\times}
.
Moreover, since
-P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp}\not\sim P_{\times}
, Lemma
3.44
implies that
-P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp}
is also coherent to some point of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,I}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}={\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\}
.
From

\varphi(-I)=I,\ \ \varphi(-P_{\times})=Q_{\times},\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi({\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{P_{\times}\})=\{Q_{\circ}\},

we obtain

(6.19)

\varphi(-P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp})=I.

Observe that
bP_{\times}^{\perp}
is coherent to the three points
0,P_{\times}^{\perp},-P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp}
.
Since

\varphi(0)=0,\ \ \varphi(P_{\times}^{\perp})=Q_{\circ},\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi(-P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp})=I,

we get
\varphi(bP_{\times}^{\perp})=Q_{\circ}
.
Observe that
P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp}
is coherent to the three points
P_{\times},I,bP_{\times}^{\perp}
.
Since

\varphi(P_{\times})=Q_{\times},\ \ \varphi(I)=I,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi(bP_{\times}^{\perp})=Q_{\circ},

we get

(6.20)

\varphi(P_{\times}+bP_{\times}^{\perp})=I.

A similar discussion shows

(6.21)

\varphi(P_{\times}-bP_{\times}^{\perp})=I

and

(6.22)

\varphi(-P_{\times}-bP_{\times}^{\perp})=I.

By Lemma
6.40
, every point in
[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
is coherent to some point of

\{P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}\,:\,c\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}\}\cup\{-P_{\times}+cP_{\times}^{\perp}\,:\,c\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}\}.

By (
6.19
), (
6.20
), (
6.21
), (
6.22
),
\varphi
sends this set to
\{I\}
.

On the other hand, by Claim
6.30
(see also Remark
6.31
) and (
6.20
) (resp. (
6.22
)), we see that
\varphi_{1}
(resp.
\varphi_{2}
) satisfies Condition (iii).
It follows that
\varphi({\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cap[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}})=\{Q_{\circ}\}
.
By Lemma
6.39
, every point in
[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
is coherent to some point of
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cap[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
.
Therefore, we get
\varphi(X)\in\ell_{Q_{\circ},I}
for every
X\in[-I,I]\setminus\ell_{0,P_{\times}}
.
Thus
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.
∎

Thus we have completed the proof of Proposition
6.34
.

Corollary 6.42
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
\varphi(A)=\varphi(B)
.
Then
\varphi
is degenerate.

6.3.
The case
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=1

Now let us consider a coherency preserver
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=1
.
By considering
\psi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\psi_{1}
for a pair of suitable automorphisms
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
, we may assume that
A=0
,
B=I
, and
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(I)=E_{11}
.

Proposition 6.43
.

Let
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserver with
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(I)=E_{11}
.
Then
\varphi
is degenerate.

The proof of this proposition is involved, so we will separate it into claims, as usual.
We assume that
\varphi\colon[0,I]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
is a coherency preserver with
\varphi(0)=0
,
\varphi(I)=E_{11}
.
Observe that
\varphi
is not standard because
0\not\sim I
and
\varphi(0)\sim\varphi(I)
.
By Corollary
3.54
, we see that
\varphi
is not standard on every nonempty open subset of
[0,I]
.
Set
\ell:=\ell_{0,E_{11}}
.
Note that
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset\ell_{\varphi(0),\varphi(I)}=\ell
holds.

Claim 6.44
.

If
\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\subset\ell
, then
\varphi([0,I])
is contained in a cone and
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
satisfy
\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\subset\ell
.
Assume in addition that there is a point
A\in(0,I)
such that
\varphi(A)\notin\mathcal{C}_{0}\cup\mathcal{C}_{E_{11}}
.
Let
A^{\prime}
be the unique point in
\ell
such that
\varphi(A)\sim A^{\prime}
.
Note that
\mathcal{S}_{0,A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{A,I}
are both contained in
[0,I]\setminus(0,I)
.
Since
\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\subset\ell
, we have
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A})=\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{A,I})=\{A^{\prime}\}
.
Thus, the restriction of
\varphi
to
[0,A]
and that to
[A,I]
both satisfy (o).
It follows that
\varphi([0,A]\cup[A,I])\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{A^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, we see from Lemma
3.44
that every element of
[0,I]\setminus([0,A)\cup(A,I])
is coherent to some point of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,A}
.
Since
\varphi({\mathcal{S}}_{0,A})=\{A^{\prime}\}
, we get
\varphi([0,I]\setminus([0,A)\cup(A,I]))\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{A^{\prime}}
.
Thus we have shown that
\varphi([0,I])\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{A^{\prime}}
in this case.

It remains to consider the case where

\varphi([0,I])\subset\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(0)}\cup\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(I)}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\cup\mathcal{C}_{E_{11}}.

If every
X\in(0,I)
satisfies
\varphi(X)\in\mathcal{C}_{E_{11}}
, then the assumption
\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\subset\ell
implies
\varphi([0,I])\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}
, as desired.
Therefore, we assume that there is an element
X\in(0,I)
satisfying
\varphi(X)\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{E_{11}}
.
For any
W\in\mathcal{S}_{X,I}
, we have
\varphi(W)\in\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\subset\ell
.
Since
0
is the unique point on the line
\ell
that is coherent to
\varphi(X)
, we conclude that

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{X,I})=\{0\}
.
Thus Proposition
6.6
implies that
\varphi
satisfies Condition (o), and we see that
\varphi([X,I])\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
We claim that

(6.23)

\varphi([0,I]\setminus[0,X))\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}.

Indeed, we already know that
\varphi(Z)\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
whenever
Z\in[X,I]
. For
Z\in[0,I]\setminus([0,X)\cup[X,I])
, we can apply Lemma
3.44
together with
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{X,I})=\{0\}
to get
\varphi(Z)\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

We show that every
Y\in[0,X)
satisfies
\varphi(Y)\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
Assume contrarily that
\varphi(Y)\notin\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
Then
\varphi(Y)\in\mathcal{C}_{E_{11}}\setminus\ell
.
Observe that
0\leq Y<X<I
and thus Corollary
3.19
implies
\mathcal{S}_{Y,X}\subset[0,I]
.
By (
6.23
), we have

(6.24)

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{Y,X})\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(Y)}=\mathcal{S}_{0,\varphi(Y)}.

On the other hand,
\mathcal{S}_{Y,I}\subset[0,I]\setminus(0,I)
implies

(6.25)

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{Y,I})\in\ell\cap\mathcal{C}_{\varphi(Y)}=\{E_{11}\}.

Since every point of
\mathcal{S}_{Y,X}
is coherent to some point of
\mathcal{S}_{Y,I}
, (
6.24
) and (
6.25
) lead to

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{Y,X})\subset\mathcal{S}_{0,\varphi(Y)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}=\{E_{11}\}.

This contradicts
\varphi(X)\not\sim E_{11}
.
Thus we get
\varphi([0,I])\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
∎

Therefore, in what follows we assume that
\varphi([0,I]\setminus(0,I))\not\subset\ell
.
By considering the mapping
X\mapsto E_{11}-\varphi(I-X)
instead of
\varphi
, if necessary, we may and do assume that there is
A\in{\mathcal{C}}_{I}\cap[0,I]
such that
\varphi(A)\notin\ell
.
Since
\varphi({\mathcal{P}})\subset\ell
, we get
0<A
.
We will apply Proposition
6.6
to the restriction
\varphi_{1}
of
\varphi
to
[0,A]
.

Claim 6.45
.

If
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (o), then
\varphi([0,I])
is contained in one cone and
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (o).
There is a singleton
\{Q\}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A})=\{Q\}
and
\varphi([0,A])\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}
.
By Lemma
3.44
, every element of
[0,I]\setminus[0,A]
is coherent to some element of
\mathcal{S}_{0,A}
.
It follows from
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A})=\{Q\}
that
\varphi([0,I]\setminus[0,A])\subset\mathcal{C}_{Q}
.
∎

Claim 6.46
.

If
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A})\subset\ell
, then
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (o), so
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A})\subset\ell
.
Let
C\in\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\,(\subset[0,I])
.
Because
\varphi(C)\sim\varphi(A)
and
\varphi(C)\in\ell
, we have
\varphi(C)=E_{11}
.
Thus
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (o).
∎

In what follows, we assume that
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A})\not\subset\ell
.
Then there is
B\in\mathcal{S}_{0,A}
such that
\varphi(B)\notin\ell
.
Observe that
E_{11}
(resp.
0
) is the unique point on
\ell
that is coherent to
\varphi(A)
(resp.
\varphi(B)
).
Let
E\in\mathcal{P}
be the unique projection satisfying
A\sim E
.
Then we have
E\sim A,0,I
, which implies
\varphi(E)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(A)}\cap\ell=\{E_{11}\}
.
Similarly, for the unique projection
F\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
B\sim F
, we have
F\sim B,0,I
, and thus
\varphi(F)=0
.

Lemma 6.47
.

Let
E,F\in{\mathcal{P}}
,
E\not=F
.
Then there exists an automorphism
\psi
of
{\overline{H_{2}}}
satisfying
\psi([0,I])=[0,I]
,
\psi(0)=0
,
\psi(I)=I
,
\psi(E)=E
, and
\psi(F)=E^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{0}
be the automorphism
X\mapsto I-(I+X)^{-1}
.
Take an invertible
2\times 2
complex matrix
T
such that
TET^{\ast}=E
and
TFT^{\ast}=E^{\perp}
.
Then the automorphism
X\mapsto\psi_{0}(T\psi_{0}^{-1}(X)T^{\ast})
satisfies the desired properties.
(See also Lemmas
6.16
,
6.17
and their proofs.)
∎

Take an automorphism
\psi
as in this lemma.
By considering
\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}
instead of
\varphi
, we may and do assume that
F=E^{\perp}
without loss of generality.
In this case, we have
A=E+cE^{\perp}
for some
c\in(0,1)
, which implies
B=cE^{\perp}
.

Claim 6.48
.

If
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (i), then
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\{A\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, and thus
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Recall that
A=E+cE^{\perp}
.
Suppose that
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (i).
Then we have
\varphi([0,A))=\{\varphi(0)\}=\{0\}
.
In particular, we have
\varphi(tE)=0
for every
t\in[0,1)
.
Therefore, Lemma
6.2
implies that
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{E,I})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
It remains to consider an element of the form
E+tE^{\perp}
,
t\in[0,1]\setminus\{c\}
.
If
t\in[0,c)
, then
E+tE^{\perp}\sim tE^{\perp}\in[0,A)
and hence we get
\varphi(E+tE^{\perp})\sim\varphi(tE^{\perp})=0
.
Let
t>c

and assume that
\varphi(E+tE^{\perp})\not\sim 0
.
Then Lemma
6.32
implies that
\varphi
restricted to
[0,E+tE^{\perp}]
also satisfies (i).
(Note that a coherency preserver satisfying (i) cannot satisfy (j) for
j\in\{o,ii,iii,iv\}
.)
It follows that
\varphi([0,E+tE^{\perp}]\setminus\{E+tE^{\perp}\})\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}
, which contradicts the facts
A\in[0,E+tE^{\perp}]\setminus\{E+tE^{\perp}\}
and
\varphi(A)\not\sim 0
.
Therefore, we get
\varphi(E+tE^{\perp})\sim 0
.
Thus we have shown that
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\{A\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
∎

Claim 6.49
.

The mapping
\varphi_{1}
never satisfies (ii).

Proof.

Recall that
A=E+cE^{\perp}
and
B=cE^{\perp}
.
Suppose that
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (ii). We are going to get a contradiction.
We have
\varphi((0,A])=\{\varphi(A)\}
. In particular, we get
\varphi(tE+cE^{\perp})=\varphi(A)
for every
t\in(0,1]
.
If
t\in(0,1]
, then
tE+E^{\perp}
is coherent to
tE+cE^{\perp}
.
Consequently,

\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})\in\ell_{\varphi(E^{\perp}),\varphi(I)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(A)}=\ell\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(A)}=\{E_{11}\}.

Hence

(6.26)

\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})=E_{11}.

By Lemma
6.2
, every point of
[0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,E^{\perp}}
is coherent to
tE+E^{\perp}
for some
t\in(0,1]
.
Since
\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{B\}\subset[0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,E^{\perp}}
, (
6.26
) implies

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{B\})\subset\mathcal{S}_{\varphi(0),\varphi(A)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}=\{E_{11}\}.

Thus we obtain
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{B\})=\{E_{11}\}
, contradicting our assumption that
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (ii).
∎

From now on, let us also consider the restriction
\varphi_{2}
of
\varphi
to
[B,I]
.
By imitating the above argument, one may complete the proof of Proposition
6.43
whenever we assume that
\varphi_{2}
satisfies (o), (i), or (ii).
Therefore, let us consider the case where
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (iii) or (iv) and
\varphi_{2}
also satisfies (iii) or (iv).
Since
E,B\in\mathcal{S}_{0,A}
and
E_{11}=\varphi(E)\neq\varphi(B)
, we have two possibilities:
Either
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\})=\{\varphi(B)\}
, or
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{B\})=\{\varphi(E)\}=\{E_{11}\}
.
Similarly, either
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{B,I}\setminus\{{E^{\perp}}\})=\{\varphi(A)\}
, or
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{B,I}\setminus\{A\})=\{\varphi(E^{\perp})\}=\{0\}
holds.

Claim 6.50
.

If
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\})=\{\varphi(B)\}
, then
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{E,I})\subset\ell_{0,\varphi(B)}
, hence
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\})=\{\varphi(B)\}
.
Let
t\in[0,1)
.
By Lemma
3.44
and the relation
tE+E^{\perp}\not\sim E
, we see that
tE+E^{\perp}
is coherent to some point in
\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\}
.
This together with
E^{\perp}\sim tE+E^{\perp}\sim I
and

\varphi(E^{\perp})=0,\ \ \varphi(I)=E_{11},\ \ \varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\})=\{\varphi(B)\}

shows that
\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})\in\ell_{0,E_{11}}\cap\ell_{0,\varphi(B)}=\{0\}

and hence

\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})=0
.

Observe that
tE+cE^{\perp}
is coherent to the three points
A,B
, and
tE+E^{\perp}
.
It follows that

\varphi(tE+cE^{\perp})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(A)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(B)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(tE+cE^{\perp})}=\ell_{\varphi(A),\varphi(B)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{0}=\{\varphi(B)\}.

Since
tE
is coherent to the three points
0,E
, and
tE+cE^{\perp}
, we get

\varphi(tE)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(0)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(E)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})}=\ell\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(B)}=\{0\}.

By Lemma
6.2
, every point of
[0,I]\setminus\ell_{E,I}
is coherent to
tE
for some
t\in[0,1)
.
Thus we get
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{E,I})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.

To complete the proof, we show that
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{E,I})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\varphi(B)}
.
It suffices to verify that every point of
[0,I]\setminus\ell_{E,I}
is coherent to some point of

(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\})\cup\{tE+cE^{\perp}\,:\,t\in[0,1)\}.

Lemma
6.2
implies that every element of
[0,A)=[0,E+cE^{\perp})
is coherent to
tE+cE^{\perp}
for some
t\in[0,1)
.
So, let
X\in[0,I]\setminus(\ell_{E,I}\cup[0,A))
.
Lemma
6.1
implies that
X\not\sim E
.
Because
X\not>A
and
X\geq 0
, Lemma
3.44
yields that
X
is coherent to some point in
\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{E\}
.
Thus we get the desired conclusion.
∎

Essentially the same argument shows that
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
whenever
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{B,I}\setminus\{{E^{\perp}}\})=\{\varphi(A)\}
.
In what follows, we consider the case where

\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{0,A}\setminus\{B\})=\{\varphi(E)\}=\{E_{11}\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi(\mathcal{S}_{B,I}\setminus\{A\})=\{\varphi(E^{\perp})\}=\{0\}

hold.

Claim 6.51
.

If
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (iii), then
\varphi_{2}
also satisfies (iii), and

\varphi([0,I]\setminus\{B\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}
.
Thus
\varphi
is of type
(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (iii).
Then we have

\varphi([0,A]\setminus\ell_{0,B})\subset\ell_{\varphi(E),\varphi(A)}=\ell_{E_{11},\varphi(A)}.

Let
t\in(0,1]
.
Since the set
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{B}\cap[0,A]\setminus\ell_{0,B})
is a singleton,
we get
\varphi(tE+cE^{\perp})=\varphi(E+cE^{\perp})=\varphi(A)
.
Observe that
tE+E^{\perp}
is coherent to three points
tE+cE^{\perp},E^{\perp},I
.
Since

\varphi(tE+cE^{\perp})=\varphi(A),\ \ \varphi(E^{\perp})=0,\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi(I)=E_{11},

we get
\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})\in\ell_{E_{11},\varphi(A)}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{0}=\{E_{11}\}
.

We now prove that
\varphi_{2}
does not satisfy (iv).
Assume on the contrary that
\varphi_{2}
satisfies (iv).
Then we have

\varphi([B,I]\setminus\ell_{A,I})\subset\ell_{\varphi(E^{\perp}),\varphi(B)}=\ell_{0,\varphi(B)}.

However, we already know that
\varphi((1/2)E+cE^{\perp})=\varphi(A)\notin\ell_{0,\varphi(B)}
although
(1/2)E+cE^{\perp}\in[B,I]\setminus\ell_{A,I}
, so we obtain a contradiction.
Thus
\varphi_{2}
satisfies (iii).
It follows that for every
t\in[0,1]
, the set
\varphi(\mathcal{C}_{tE+cE^{\perp}}\cap[B,I]\setminus\ell_{B,A})
is a singleton.
This together with Lemma
6.2
and the fact that
\varphi(tE+E^{\perp})=E_{11}
for every
t\in(0,1]
yields
\varphi((B,I])=\{E_{11}\}
.

We show that
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\{B\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}
.
By Lemma
6.2
, we see that every element of
[0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,E^{\perp}}
is coherent to
tE+E^{\perp}
for some
t\in(0,1]
. Thus
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{0,E^{\perp}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}
.
If
t\in(c,1]
, then
tE^{\perp}\sim E+tE^{\perp}\in(B,I]
, thus
\varphi(tE^{\perp})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{E_{11}}
.
It remains to show that
\varphi(tE^{\perp})\sim E_{11}
for every
t\in[0,c)
.
Assume towards a contradiction that
\varphi(tE^{\perp})\not\sim E_{11}
for some
t\in[0,c)
.
Since
\varphi
restricted to
[B,I]
satisfies (iii) and does not satisfy (iv), Lemma
6.33
implies that
\varphi
restricted to
[tE^{\perp},I]
needs to satisfy (iii).
In particular, we see that
\varphi((tE^{\perp},I])
is necessarily contained in a line.
However, it is easily seen that
\{I\}\cup({\mathcal{S}}_{B,I}\setminus\{E^{\perp}\})\subset(tE^{\perp},I]
and
that
\varphi(\{I\}\cup({\mathcal{S}}_{B,I}\setminus\{E^{\perp}\}))=\{E_{11},\varphi(A),0\}
is not contained in a line.
Thus we get to a contradiction.
∎

Similarly, if
\varphi_{2}
satisfies (iv), then so does
\varphi_{1}
, and
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\{A\})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
Let us finish the proof of Proposition
6.43
by considering the remaining case.

Claim 6.52
.

If
\varphi_{1}
satisfies (iv) and
\varphi_{2}
satisfies (iii), then
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{A,B})\subset\ell
, so
\varphi
is of type
(\ell)
.

Proof.

From the assumption, we obtain
\varphi([0,A]\setminus\ell_{A,B})\subset\ell
and
\varphi([B,I]\setminus\ell_{A,B})\subset\ell
.
We also see that
\varphi(tE^{\perp})=\{0\}
for every
t\in[0,c)
and
\varphi(E+tE^{\perp})=\{E_{11}\}
for every
t\in(c,1]
.

We see that each element of
[0,I]\setminus[0,A]
is coherent to
E+tE^{\perp}
for some
t\in(c,1]
.
Indeed, if
X\in{\mathcal{C}}_{A}\cap[0,I]\setminus[0,A]
, then
X=A+R
for some
R
of rank at most one, and
X\not\leq A
implies
R\geq 0
, which yields
A\leq X\leq I
and hence
X\sim I
.
If
X\in[0,I]\setminus([0,A]\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{A})
, then
\det(X-A)\not=0
. Because neither
X<A
nor
X>A
, we have
\det(X-A)<0
. This together with
\det(X-I)\geq 0
and
the intermediate value theorem shows the existence of
t\in(c,1]
with
X\sim E+tE^{\perp}
.
Similarly, each element of
[0,I]\setminus[B,I]
is coherent to
tE^{\perp}
for some
t\in[0,c)
.
Thus we obtain
\varphi([0,I]\setminus([0,A]\cup[B,I]))\subset\ell
.
Therefore, we get
\varphi([0,I]\setminus\ell_{A,B})\subset\ell
.
∎

Corollary 6.53
.

Let
A,B\in H_{2}
satisfy
A<B
.
Let
\varphi\colon[A,B]\to{\overline{H_{2}}}
be a coherency preserving map with
d(\varphi(A),\varphi(B))=1
.
Then
\varphi
is degenerate.

Theorem B is the union of Corollaries
6.10
,
6.42
,
6.53
.
